1327. THOMAS HALL was indicted for stealing, on the 25th of April, 2 table-spoons, value 30s., the goods of Henry Richard Elsworth. - To which he pleaded GUILTY. Aged 22. - Confined Nine Months.
[trialAccount t18320517-202]
1328. JOHN WILLIAM HARTAM was indicted for that he, on the 27th of April, did forge a certain order for the delivery of goods, which is as follows:-
To the Directors of the West India Dock Company.
GENTLEMEN, - Please to deliver to order of Mr. Charles Kendall, nine hundred coker-nuts, ex Othello, under my command, from Honduras, entered by Pickstock, 24th of April, 1832.
JOHN BISHOP, Master and Consignee. with intent to defraud John Bishop; against the Statute,& c.
FIVE OTHER COUNTS, varying the manner of laying the charge.
CHARLES KENDALL. I am a Custom-house agent, and live at York-terrace, Stepney. The prisoner was introduced to me about the 27th of April, by Mr. William Hallett, who said he was captain of the Othello, or words to that effect, and that he had some cokernuts to sell; I told him if he had coker-nuts to sell, I would give him the best price for them, and I would go on board the following morning or the morning after, and look at them - I went the next morning but one, and saw the prisoner, who appeared in treaty with another person; I agreed to purchase the coker-nuts at 17s. a hundred - I came round to the baggage-warehouse with him, where I saw my clerk, and I left him to close the transaction.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q.Is Mr. Hallett here? A. Not that I am aware of; he introduced the prisoner as master or captain - we have but one meaning for those words in the merchant service; Mr. Hallett is a Custom-house agent - I saw him some days ago; the prisoner speaks English, but I observed he was a foreigner; I think I saw Mr. Hallett at the Police-office.
THOMAS PICKSTOCK. I am a merchant, and live in Nicholas-lane. I have the bills of lading; the prisoner was acquainted with the captain, who is not here - he was employed by the captain to sell those coker-nuts; I believe, on my oath, that he has acted as an innocent man, under the authority of the captain.
NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18320517-203]
1329. ELLEN REED was indicted for stealing, on the 20th of April, 1 gown, value 1s. 6d.; 6 spoons, value 20s., and 1 pair of trousers, value 2s., the goods of Robert Cussen.
ROBERT CUSSEN. I keep a chandler's shop in Robin Hood-lane, Poplar. The prisoner lodged at my house by the name of Mrs. Fisher, with a person who either was or passed as her husband - she left us on the 20th of March, and left some goods there; on the 10th of April she came again, and asked to be allowed to go to the room which she had occupied, and I went with her - it was then about half-past five o'clock; I had six silver tea-spoons laying on a table in that room - I saw them distinctly; I left her in the room about ten minutes; she then came down and went out; in about an hour I went to the room again, and missed the spoons - there was nobody in the house but me; my wife afterwards told me her gown was gone, and on the same day I missed a pair of trousers.
MICHAEL DRISCOLL (Police-constable H 133.) In consequence of information I went to a house in Gloucester-row, Chelsea; I found the prisoner in a lodging there - this gown was laying on the bed-post; I asked if it was not Mrs. Cussen's - she said it was; I took her and told her she was accused of stealing six tea-spoons and a pair of trousers - she denied all knowledge of them, but said Mrs. Cussen gave her the gown.
SUSANNAH CUSSEN. I am the prosecutor's wife. This is my gown - I never lent it, sold it, or gave it to the prisoner - I had it in my own bed-room a fortnight before she quitted; and five or six days after St. Patrick's-day I missed it.
Prisoner. She gave it me when I was going to an Irish club. Witness. No, I never gave it - she never wore it in my presence - I am quite sure I never gave her authority to wear it; our street door was open in the day time - I do not know that another person might not have gone in, and taken the spoons; they have never been found.
Prisoner. I have witnesses to prove that I wore this gown in her house before her and her husband.
ROBERT CUSSEN. Their furniture is left there, and is, I believe, worth from 25s. to 30s. - she owed me 6l. 5s.; she did not offer to pay two sovereigns, nor did the man; I never saw her wear this gown.
Witness for the Defence.
MARY SULLIVAN. I lodged in the prosecutor's house for ten months, and never saw a silver spoon there - the prisoner lived there at the time; I saw this gown new upon Mrs. Cussen, and I afterwards saw it on the prisoner - upon my soleman oath I saw the prisoner wearing this gown for two months, while she was a lodger there.
MARGARET DONOHUE. I live at Blackwall. I have been in and out at the prosecutor's for three months in the last winter - I saw the prisoner twice with this gown on, in the presence of the prosecutor and his wife.
ROBERT CUSSEN. Sullivan left my house on the 17th of December - she had owed me a debt, which I got of her, and she has not been in my house since - Donohue has been there, but very seldom.
SUSANNAH CUSSEN. The prisoner left me three days after St. Patrick's-day - I saw my gown safe a fortnight before she left.
MARY SULLIVAN re-examined. Q. Upon your oath have you been into the prosecutor's house since the first day of the year? A. No, but I have seen the prisoner wearing the gown both in and out of the house - I live close by.
CATHERINE DRISCOLL. I used to go to the prosecutor's house to see their baby - I have seen the prisoner wear this gown; I am sure it is the same - I saw it on Mrs. Cussen's back when it was new.
NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18320517-204]
1330. JOHN CASHMAN was indicted for stealing, on the 17th of May, 1 silver spoon, value 5s., the goods of John Thurlow.
SIMON WESTLAKE. I keep the Horse Shoe, in Titchbourne-street. On the 17th of May I saw a ladder placed against a house opposite to mine, by the prisoner - the proprietor of the house came out, spoke to him, and went in again - the prisoner then ascended the ladder, went on tothe next house (No. 11), lifted up the window, went in, and returned in about a minute; I saw he was putting something into his pocket - he came down, took the ladder, and went away; I told the prosecutor of it.
THOMAS THURLOW. I am the son of John Thurlow - we live at No. 11. In consequence of what Westlake said I went in pursuit of the prisoner, and came up with him in Coventry-street - he put down the ladder in Cox-court, and was about to descend into a cellar - I collared him, and this spoon, which is my father's, was produced to me by Joseph Simms, the proprietor of the One Tun.
MARY SHADWELL. I was standing near the cellar-flap, and saw the prisoner put the ladder down - he was going down himself, when Mr. Thurlow seized him - he pulled a spoon from under his jacket; I saw Mr. Simms take it from where he shifted it to, and give it to Mr. Thurlow - this is it.
FREDERICK CULLIMORE (Police-constable C 143). I took the prisoner, and have the spoon.
Prisoner. I was very much distressed - I meant to return it.
GUILTY. Aged 38. - Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18320517-205]
1331. HENRY SAMPSON and WILLIAM HAYNES were indicted for stealing, on the 4th of May, 1 handkerchief, value 3s., the goods of Thomas Gotobed, from his person.
THOMAS GOTOBED. I live in Russell-street, Bloomsbury. On the afternoon of the 4th of May I was walking down Gate-street, Lincoln's Inn-fields; a person touched my coat pocket; I turned, and saw the two prisoners in custody of Goldstone - Sampson had my handkerchief concealed in his jacket; he gave it me himself - I delivered it to the officer.
JAMES GOLDSTONE. I am an umbrella-maker, and live in Gate-street. On the 4th of May I was in my shop, and saw the prosecutor pass, followed by the prisoners -I saw Haynes lift up the prosecutor's pocket; I left my shop to go and caution him, but before I got to him Haynes took the handkerchief, and gave it to Sampson; I took them both - by my direction Sampson gave up the handkerchief.
JOHN GALLAHER. I am a Police-constable. I took the prisoners.
(Property produced and sworn to.)

[trialAccount t18320517-206]
1332. JOHN SMITH was indicted for stealing, on the 2nd of May, 2 hats, value 26s., the goods of Samuel Dutton.
SAMUEL DUTTON. I live on Ludgate-hill, and am a hatter. I know nothing of the prisoner, but I lost these hats from my shop.
JOHN KIRKMAN (Police-constable F 107). I took the prisoner carrying these two hats in the Strand, on the 2nd of May; I asked how he got them; he said they belonged to him - I asked what was the initials on them; he declined to tell me; I took him a little way; he made a bolt, and ran; I took him again, and took him to the station - I found this model of a key upon him.
PETER GEORGE HASE. I am a shoemaker. I was going down Ludgate-hill - I saw the prisoner and a respectably dressed young man; I had seen them in bad company, and I followed them; when we were opposite to Rundell and Bridge's I missed the other; I waited a few minutes, and saw the prisoner go into the prosecutor's shop, and come out with these hats - I followed him to the Straud, and told the officer, who took him.
(Property produced and sworn to.)
GUILTY. Aged 32 - Transported for Seven Years.

1333. JAMES SMITH was indicted for stealing, on the 8th of May, 1 whip, value 4s., the goods of Arthur Williams.
ARTHUR WILLIAMS. I drive a beer-dray, and live in Wood's-place, Bowling-green-lane. On the 8th of May I left my dray in Tavistock-square; I put my whip against some rails while I delivered some beer; when I came back, I missed the whip - I saw the prisoner at a distance; he threw the whip out of his hand; my fellow-servant pursued; I went round, and met him - this is my whip; I am sure he is the person.
THOMAS GOODVER (Police-constable E 39). I took the prisoner, and produce the whip.
The prisoner put in a written Defence, pleading poverty.

[trialAccount t18320517-208]
1334. FREDERICK TERRY was indicted for stealing, on the 23rd of April, 2 half-crowns, and 1 shilling, the monies of John Auden, from his person.
JOHN AUDEN. I live with my father, in Homer-street. On the 23rd of April I was sitting at a table in Chalk Farm tea-gardens; I put some silver into my coat pocket - I saw the prisoner sitting at a table, on a form; I thought I felt his hand in my pocket; I felt, and my money was gone - I waited to see what he would do; when he was leaving the garden I accused him of it; he said he had not a farthing about him - he was searched, and two half-crowns found on him; I thought at first he did it for a joke - I had tossed with him for a glass of brandy and water, and a pint of ale.
Cross-examined by MR. PRENDERGAST. Q. How many persons were there? A. I think there were four females; two of them were named Cohen; I do not know the other's name - O yes, I forgot, one of them was named Mitchell; I do not recollect any other female who had been in my company that night - I do not know a girl named Reeves; I have seen the prisoner two or three times, but had no acquaintance with him - I had been in his company accidentally; I have met him at Mr. McDonald's concert-room, in Portland-street, and may have joked with him; I do not recollect saying to Mary Ann Mitchell that she had no need to fear any thing about a subposna, and she had better not come here - I could almost say No.
Q. Now, do you not know that you told her she had better not come here, and that she had no need to care for a subpoena, as it would not be enforced? A. I was at the Yorkshire Stingo last Sunday evening; she there told me that she had to come here - I asked what it was, she said to appear; that was all I said; I may have seen her ten times at McDonald's; she did not tell me from whom the subpoena came, nor what trial it was on Sunday, but I had heard her say at McDonald's that shehad to appear here - I do not think I told her she had no need to come; the prisoner did not say that it was true enough he had some money of mine, but he should keep it for a treat - my money did not fall out; there had not been a great deal of larking - we had brandy and water, and ale; I paid for some; I think the prisoner paid 6d., and Mitchell paid 6d.
JOHN TISDALR. I am a constable. I was going up to Chalk Farm a little after seven o'clock in the evening of the 23rd of April; I saw a mob - the prisoner was sitting on a grass plot, and the prosecutor standing by, asking him for his money - the prisoner made use of very bad language, and swore he had not got a farthing about him; I took off his shoes, and found in his stockings two half-crowns, which I took through some boles; he made a great deal of resistance - when I found the money he said he was very sorry, and wanted the prosecutor to forgive him; he said he would give him 1l. - he said he knew he should be transported, and it would be a good job for him.
Cross-examined. Q.Do you remember his saying he did not intend to steal it? A. I did not hear that; I recollect his telling the prosecutor they were all friends together - I did not notice any girls there.
JOHN GAYTON JOLLY (Police-constable S 164). I was on duty near Chalk Farm gardens - I saw the prisoner in custody of Tisdale; I assisted to take him to the watch-house - I said to him, "Don't you think you are a pretty sort of a fellow, to sit with a friend and rob him?" he said,"I did rob him, and it would be the best thing for him and for me to be transported."
Witnesses for the Defence.
MARY ANN MITCHELL. I live in Frederick-place, Hampstead-road. On the 23rd of April I was at Chalk Farm, with the prosecutor and the prisoner; I had seen the prosecutor before - I went across the gardens to speak to a friend, and saw the prisoner and him quarrelling - I asked what it was about; the prosecutor said he had lost 7s. out of his pocket, and he could not accuse any one but the prisoner; the prisoner was very much intoxicated, and could not say much to him, but kept walking round and round - we had a good deal to drink; I saw the prosecutor at the Yorkshire Stingo on Sunday week, and on last Sunday; I said I was subpoenaed to come here - he said I had no call to come without I liked, but he did not say why; I had seen him a good many times at Mr. Donald's
COURT. Q. Did you see the money found? A. No, I did not notice that - I considered it might be done out of a joke; I did not hear the prisoner deny having it.
ELLEN SCOTT. I live in Portland-street. I have known the prosecutor and the prisoner some time - I was at Chalk Farm on the 23rd of April; I saw the prosecutor, the prisoner, and several others romping on the grass; the form was knocked over; the prosecutor said he had lost 7s., but he could not blame any body but the prisoner; I did not hear him say he found his hand in his pocket; I thought they were friends.
COURT. Q.What are you? A. A servant out of place - I went to Chalk Farm that day, being Easter Monday. GUILTY. Aged 18.
Recommended to Mercy. - Confined Fourteen Days.
[trialAccount t18320517-209]
1335. WILLIAM WARE was indicted for stealing, on the 24th of March, 1 saw, value 1s. 6d., the goods of Thomas Capps; and that he had been before convicted of felony.
THOMAS CAPPS. I am a pawnbroker, and live in Old-street-road. On the 24th of March I missed a small brass-backed saw from a stall in front of my house - I had seen it safe half an hour before; it has not been found since - we had a young man to mind the property, but he turned his back for a moment.
JOSEPH ROBINS. I live opposite the prosecutor. I was at my door a little after nine o'clock in the morning; I saw a person, who has been convicted, take the saw from the board - he gave it to the prisoner, who gave it to another person.
WILLIAM ROBINS. I saw Porfleet, who has been convicted, take the saw and give it to the prisoner, and they all moved off together.
ROBERT LOVELOCK. I am an officer. I took the prisoner in Wilderness-row - he denied all knowledge of it.
CHARLES WALLER. I am a Police-constable. I have a certificate of the prisoner's former conviction, which I got at Mr. Clark's office - I was present, and know he is the person - (read.)
GUILTY. Aged 17. - Transported for Fourteen Years.

JAMES BUCKLE. I am an officer; I received the prisoner in charge.(Property produced and sworn to.)

1337. ANN WALKER was indicted for stealing, on the 28th of April, 4 lbs, weight of cheese, value 3s. 6d., the goods of Francis Bateman; and that she had been before convicted of felony.
MATTHEW BATEMAN. I am in the service of Francis Bateman - he keeps a shop in Theobald's-road. On the evening of the 28th of April I saw the prisoner come to the shop; she did not buy any thing - I missed some cheese from the window; I followed her - I saw something under her shawl; she gave it into another woman's apron; the other woman turned up a mews, and threw the cheese down, saying she did not want it - the prisoner followed her; I kept close to her; I called my brother, and she was taken - this is the cheese; here are 41bs. of it.
CHARLES NORMAN (Police-constable 48E). I took the prisoner from the witness, and have the cheese.

The prisoner pleaded poverty.

[trialAccount t18320517-212]
1338 FANNY BURR was indicted for stealing, on the 8th of May, 9 sovereigns, the monies of John Christie, from his person.
MR. ADOLPHUS conducted the prosecutions.
JOHN CHRISTIE. I was a shopkeeper, but have retired, and live in George-street, Lambeth. On the 7th of May I had been dining with some friends; I had been drinking all day, and was going home at past one o'clock in the morning - I had a little terrier dog with me; I was very heavy with liquor, and rather sleepy - I observed a woman pick the dog up; I consider it was near Charing-cross - she kept walking on; I followed her into a house in Whitcomb-street; I remember going into a room, but after that I remember no more till I was awoke; I had not picked up the woman, or made any advances to her; I had a watch, and nine sovereigns in gold, in my fob, loose, under my watch; I consider that I fell asleep immediately - I was awoke by a woman, but I cannot say who it was; she asked me something; I felt for my watch, and found my fob was turned inside out - I believe I sat still, but I hardly know; I then heard a person's voice say."Here, here, take your watch," and it was quietly put into my hand; upon this, a Policeman came up - the prisoner was then present; the Policeman asked if I had lost any thing; I said, Yes, ten or twelve sovereigns, and my watch, but the woman had given me my watch back - I went to the Police station - I fell asleep till the morning - I then went before the Magistrate, and gave my evidence; I can recollect as late as twelve o'clock at night, that I had ten sovereigns in my watch-fob, and I took out one, and changed it; I had changed two in the course of the day; from twelve o'clock till one I had been at a public-house in Jermyn-street.
Cross-examined by MR. LEE. Q. Is this house one that you know? A. I lived so long in the neighbourhood I cannot say I do not know the street, there are several brothels in that street; I had never been in that house, to my knowledge, before; the dog caused me to go there - I know I had my money at twelve o'clock, when I got change for a sovereign - I then took out my watch, and felt the rest of my money there; my sovereigns could not fall out - my fob is very narrow - I swear I had nine sovereigns at twelve o'clock at night; in the morning, when I came from Lambeth to town, I know perfectly well I had twelve sovereigns in my watch fob; I changed no more than three - I had been drinking all day; I can drink a good deal sometimes - I had part of two bottles of wine between three of us before breakfast; I had wine and water at different houses- I think I was in four houses - I drank no beer; I might take ale, or a glass of rum and water - after two o'clock I became very much intoxicated; I took two glasses of rum and water with a friend in Jermyn-street, and do not recollect what took place after that.
MR. ADOLPHUS. Q. Did you know the prisoner before? A.No.
ANN BARRETT. I am servant at the house, No. 18, Whitcomb-street. The prisoner did not lodge there, but on the 8th of May she came there, about half-past one o'clock in the morning; she had a dog in her arms - the prosecutor followed her, and asked for the dog; she was going up stairs - I told her the prosecutor was in liquor; she said he was not in liquor, but near sighted - they then went up stairs, and were there for an hour or an hour and a half; I then heard a creaking on the stairs - I went out of the parlour, and saw the prisoner coming down the last flight of stairs, with her shoes in her hand; I asked where the gentleman was - she said he was up stairs, that he had but 2s. about him, and she would not stop with him; I told her to go up stairs - I followed her with a candle; when I went into the room I found the prosecutor leaning back on the sofa, with his hat under the stove, and his fobpocket turned inside out, which was not so when he went in; I said to the prisoner,
"I don't think it is all right;" she said she knew the prosecutor, that he was a horse-dealer, and lived in Tothill-street - I shook the prosecutor, and awoke him; the prisoner was standing by - I told him she had been leaving the house without her shoes, and I had detained her; he put his hand to his fob, and said he had lost his watch - I then came down to call a Policeman, but my husband had called one, and he went up with him; the prisoner had fastened the room door - the Policeman knocked, and she opened the door; there was then a sovereign and two halfpence on the table, which had not been there just before - and the prosecutor was sitting at the foot of the bed, with his watch in his hand; he told us the woman had given him the watch back - the officer then asked him if he had lost any thing; he said he had lost ten or twelve sovereigns - the prisoner declared she had not more than half a crown or two shillings about her, and wished to be searched in the house; the prosecutor and the prisoner were then taken to St. Ann's watch-house - the prosecutor seemed very much in liquor, and more so when I went up to awake him than before.
Cross-examined. Q. Are you married? A. Yes, and I live servant there; there was no one in the house but the prosecutor, the prisoner, I, my husband, and my master.
WILLIAM BARRETT. I am the husband of this witness. I saw the prisoner come in about half-past one o'clock, with a dog in her arms; she went up stairs, and the prosecutor went up after her - I heard her say to him on the stairs,"Do you mean to stop all night or not?" he said,
"No, I want my dog;" they went up stairs, and I heard no more for some time; I then heard some footsteps coming down stairs very slow and very light - I came out of the room, and saw the prisoner with her shoes in her hand; my wife stopped her; I went for the Policeman, and went up stairs with him - he knocked at the door, and sang out"Police - if you don't open the door I will;" I went in, and saw the prosecutor sitting on the bed, with his watch in his hand, and his fob turned out; there was a sovereign and two halfpence on the table - the prisoner said she had nothing about her whatever, and the Policeman took her in charge.
Cross-examined. Q. Did you or your wife go out first when you heard the prisoner? A.We both went out together - she denied that she had any money of the prosecutor's.
COURT. Q. Give us her words? A. I asked the prosecutor if he had lost any money, and he said, Yes, ten or twelve sovereigns; the prisoner said she had no money belonging to him, and that she had no money at all about her - I and my wife were sitting in the back parlour when she came down - I went for the Policeman; my wife did not go out of the street door.
WILLIAM TURNER (Police-constable C 176.) I wascalled into the house by William Barrett - we went up stairs; the room door was fast - I knocked and called out Police! I heard a lock or a bolt move; I then opened the door, which I could not do before, and went in - I saw the prosecutor sitting on the foot of the bed; I said,
"What is the matter, old boy?" and tapped him on the shoulder; I saw he was very tipsy - he said,
"I have lost my watch and my sovereigns;" I said, "You have your watch in your hand" - he said, "She gave it me back;" I said,"How many sovereigns have you lost?" he said ten or twelve - the prisoner said,
"I have no money belonging to him;" I said,
"Here is a sovereign on the table" - she said, "I would not submit to him for a sovereign;" I said,
"You are very extravagant, I will take this and put it into my pocket - come, turn it up, or I will take you to the station;" she said,
"Search me here" - I said, "No, I will take you to the station;" we went there - I said to the inspector, "The old gentleman is not quite compos mentis, but he tells his story so straight forward that I wish to search the prisoner;" I took a candle and went into a private cell, where I stripped the prisoner to her shift, stockings, and shoes - two sovereigns fell from her bosom, and she said,
"They were given me by a gentleman I met in the Haymarket;" she then said I was a very good lady's maid, and asked me to dress her again - I said I would do my best; she said, "You have found nothing," but I told her to sit down, and I took off one of her shoes - she again said, "You have found nothing," but I took off the other, and found these six sovereigns in it.
Cross-examined. Q.Have you not a woman for the purpose of examining females? A.No; the prisoner made no charge against the prosecutor; she said she had no money belonging to him, and no money at all.
Prisoner's Defence. They did not ask me if I had any money, only if I had robbed the prosecutor; I told them I had not - I had met him that night near Charing-cross; he asked me to take him home, and I said I could not; he then asked me if I knew a house; I took him to Whitcomb-street - he said he was afraid of losing his dog - I said I would take that; he told me to go on, and he would follow me; we went to a back room, for which he paid 1s. - he gave me two sovereigns, which I put into my bosom; he then wanted to take improper liberties with me - I was an hour and a half before I could get away; I was going down, and met the witness on the stairs; she said, "Where is your friend;" I said,
"He is up stairs;" he had sat down, and pretended to be asleep - the officer was fetched, and I was taken to the station; the next day he did not know what to say; he at first said it was at half-past two o'clock, then at three, and then between eleven and twelve; the officer told him what to say.
ANN BARRETT re-examined. Q.Had the prosecutor paid 1s. for the room? A. Yes, he certainly had; she forced the 1s. out of his pocket, and he said, "I want my dog, I want to go home."
GUILTY. Aged 30. - Transported for Fourteen Years.
[trialAccount t18320517-213]
1339. WILLIAM JOHNSON was indicted for stealing, on the 18th of April, 1 Bible, value 15s., the goods of John Stenson.
THOMAS HENRY PARKER. I live at my father-in-law's shop, in Princes-street, Soho; he is a bookseller. On Wednesday evening the 18th of April, a person came in very like the prisoner, in a blue cloak, like the one here produced; he asked for a Tasso, or some such book which he had seen at our house - I told him we had no such book; I had observed a Bible on the counter that day - he said he would call again the next morning; I saw the Bible which had been on our counter at Mr. Barron's on the Tuesday following - this is it; it has our mark on it; I had not sold it - it is worth 15s.
Prisoner. This witness said at the office that he did not recognize me in the slightest degree; he was urged by the Police-officer to say that I was the man, and that I was well known - and then he said I was something like the thief. Witness. I said I did not recollect your face exactly; I had not seen the cloak produced before I saw you - I said before I recollected, that it was on Wednesday or Thursday, but I afterwards recollected it was Wednesday, two days before Good Friday. I did not say it was a wet evening, and that the prisoner was muffled up in a cloak; I said he had a cloak on - it was between eight and nine o'clock, as near as I can recollect; it was dark; the shop was not very well lighted; you was not there many minutes; there was no other person present - I missed the Bible in about half an hour - I do not recollect selling you a book about a fortnight before; I cannot recollect your face now, but your voice is similar - the Bible has a mark in it, but some dirt has been rubbed over it.
JAMES BARRON. I live in Drury-lane, and keep a general sale shop. On Wednesday evening, the 18th of April, the prisoner came in; he had a blue cloth coat and spectacles on; he was very smartly dressed, and his cloak enveloped him completely; the prisoner is the man, I recollect his person perfectly well - he came about ten minutes or a quarter-past nine o'clock; he said, "Is this a pawnbroker's?" I said "No, it is not;" he said, "I have a book I want to raise some money on," and he asked if I would buy it; I said I did not care; he showed me this Bible, said it was valuable, and had the Apocrypha - he wanted 10s. on it - I gave him 8s. for it, and I said if he wanted it again he should have it; he pressed very much upon me to keep it for him; I put it in the window the next day - I particularly noticed his voice, he has rather a foreign accent and I recognized him by that; Mr. Parker came and asked for the book, and knew it by the mark; the prisoner came again on the Tuesday evening following, with a brush; I had received information to detain him; I made sure he was the man, and I called my wife to be sure that he was the person - she said Yes; I have not the slightest doubt by his voice and appearance that he is the man - this is the Bible; it had a private mark in it, which had been rubbed when I had it from the prisoner; I think my shop is three-quarters of a mile from the prosecutor's.
THOMAS WRAY (Police-constable F 31). I was called into Mr. Barron's shop, to take the prisoner; he had a blue coat and silver spectacles; he had a crape hat-band on, but he took it off in the station-house, while I went for Miss Roberts.
Prisoner. When you returned you said I had taken off the crape, which I had rolled up, that it might resume a smooth shape - I did it from no sinister motive.
THOMAS HENRY PARKER. I have not the slightestdoubt that this book is ours - we had exactly such a one; I did not observe whether the prisoner had spectacles on, he had a black hat.
Prisoner. During the whole of the evening I was in the company of a young gentleman whose mother is present, but I am told he is not at present to be found; I returned to my apartments at ten o'clock, and left them at four o'clock, I was never from the society nor from the arm of that person; in the deposition Barron distincly swore the Bible was sold to him on the Thursday, and it would have been impossible for me to have known whether it was a valuable one, and whether it had the Apocrypha or not in it, if I had it so short a time.
GUILTY. Aged 32.
1340. WILLIAM JOHNSON was again indicted for stealing, on the 24th of April, 1 hat-brush, value 2s., the goods of William Roberts.
JAMES BARRON. The prisoner came to my house on the 24th of April with this brush - he was dressed in black and had a blue cloak on his arm, and his spectacles as before - I looked at him, as I had received information but half an hour before - I said to him,
"You sold me a book?" he said, "I did;" I said, "I have had information, and I must detain you;" he said, "For God's sake, don't do that, I shall be ruined;" I said, "I must do my duty;" I sent my servant for an officer, but Wray was going by, and he took him - I looked at the brush, but did not particularly observe it.
ELIZABETH ROBERTS. I live with my father, William Roberts - he lives in St. Martin's-court, and is a brush-maker. On the 24th of April a man came into our shop, and asked to look at a clothes-brush; I took out the drawer- he said he wanted one with a handle; I turned, and got one out of the window - he said the hairs were not long enough; I said we had no longer, and he went out - we had several brushes on a tray in the shop; I did not miss one, but I have seen the one produced - it has my father's name on it, and had been in our shop; it is worth 3s. - I had not sold such a brush on the 24th of April - the person had a cloak on his arm, a stick in his hand, and a crape hat-band - I did not observe his face or his voice; it was between six and seven o'clock in the evening.
THOMAS WRAY. I am the officer. I took the prisoner about nine o'clock in Mr. Barron's shop - I took the brush from his hand at the station; he had this cloak and this stick - I do not know whether he had any spectacles on, as I did not like to take hold of him - I thought he was certainly a nobleman; he then had whiskers, and his hair was all about his forehead, but he was shaved his whiskers off.
Prisoner. I have a witness that I bought that brush in the beginning of Passion-week, in St. Martin's-court - I believe it was of Miss Roberts herself, though it was only the affair of a moment.
ELIZABETH HASLET. I live in White Lion-street, Pentonville - the prisoner lodged with me. I know he had a brush of this kind - he showed it to me, and said he gave 2s. 9d. for it; he lived with me five or six weeks - I cannot say when I first saw the brush, nor when I last saw it- it was a small hat-brush.
GUILTY. Aged 32. - Transported for Fourteen Years.
[trialAccount t18320517-214]
1341. RICHARD GIBBS was indicted for stealing, on the 6th of May, 1 bottle, value 3d., and 1 pint of rum, value 2s., the goods of Charles Temple.
ALGERNON SMITH (Police-constable S 22). On the night of the 7th of May I stopped the prisoner in Skinner-street, with this bottle under his coat; he appeared partly intoxicated - I asked where he got it - he said he bought it of a boy for 4d., at the corner of Brill-row.
CHARLES TEMPLE. I keep the Somers'-town coffee-house, in Charlton-street . I have lost two or three sample-bottles of spirits - this is one of mine, and had rum in it; it has the mark of the ship on it: it was safe on the Friday, when we cleaned the window.
Prisoner. He could not swear to it at the office. Witness. My young man could not swear to it, but when I went I could - he was taken nearly a quarter of a mile from my house.
JURY. Q. Are you satisfied that no other publican has such a private mark as you have? A. It is not my mark, it is the ship's mark, and my number, which is 63; the numbers go regularly.
GUILTY. Aged 18. - Transported for Seven Years.

1342. JAMES EPPS was indicted for stealing, on the 9th of May, 1 fish-plate, value 2s., the goods of John Walden.
ELIZABETH WALDEN. I live with my father, John Walden, at Somers'-town - he deals in china. I saw the prisoner outside the shop, on the morning of the 9th of May; he asked the price of something - I said I could not tell, as my mother was out; I then saw a fish-tray under his arm; I went and overtook him in Seymour-street, with it - he said he had bought it of a girl, but gave it to me; it is the same size and pattern as ours, and we missed one - it has no mark on it.
FREDERICK HILES. I took the prisoner - he told me he bought the plate of a girl for 3d. - he did not say when.
Prisoner's Defence. I bought it in Petticoat-lane.
NOT GUILTY.

1343 HENRY COTTON was indicted for stealing, on the 5th of May, 1 handkerchief, value 4s., the goods of Thomas Jackson, from his person.
THOMAS JACKSON. I live in George-street, Minories. On the 5th of May, about half-past ten o'clock at night, I was walking along; I felt something - I turned and missed my handkerchief; I saw the prisoner a few yards off, throwing it down - I pursued him, and he was stopped; I had not lost sight of him - this is it.
JOHN DAGLEY (Police-constable G 171). I stopped the prisoner at the corner of Greville-street, Hatton-garden- I got this handkerchief from a young lady, in the prosecutor's presence: I saw the prisoner throw two handkerchiefs down, and I got another from him at the station.
Prisoner's Defence. I never had the prosecutor's handkerchief in my possession; I did not throw any away- the gentleman raised his umbrella to strike me, and I ran away.
GUILTY. Aged 20. - Transported for Fourteen Years.

1344. WILLIAM DAY was indicted for stealing, on the 6th of May, 2 shillings, the monies of Henry Vickress, his master.
ELIZA GRIGGS. I am in the service of Mr. Henry Vickress - he keeps the Pied Bull, in the Lower-road, Islington - the prisoner is his porter. In consequence of something that occurred. I placed 20s. in silver in the till, which had been marked by Mr. Vickress, and given to me- a little after six o'clock in the morning on the 6th of May, the prisoner had been cleaning the bar, and I missed two shillings - when Mr. Vickress came down I told her of it; the officer was called, and these two shillings were found on him; I know they are two of the shillings which had been in the till - they have the marks on them.
GEORGE COLLIER (Police-constable N 94). I was called in, and took the prisoner - I found the two marked shillings in his waistcoat pocket.
HENRY VICKRESS. The prisoner was in my employ. -I marked these two shillings, and know them again.
The prisoner received a good character, and his master engaged to employ him again.
GUILTY. Aged 35. - Confined Seven Days.

1345. SARAH GOLDING was indicted for stealing, on the 9th of May, 2 pairs of gloves, value 1s.; 12 yards of quilling, value 7d.; 1 yard of ribbon, value 4d., and 3 yards of lace, value 2s. 9d., the goods of Richard Hall and another.
CHARLES WARMALL. I am shopman to Richard Hall and another, linen-draper s, St. John-street. On the 9th of May the prisoner came and bought some quilling net, gloves, and stockings, which came to 1s. 10d. - I saw her conceal these articles, which were worth 4s. 9d.; she handed them to her little boy, who is about seven years old, and he put them into her basket; she took them out and put them into her pocket - I let her go out, and as she passed the window I took her back; my master told her to give up what she had - she took them out and put them down.
CHARLES WRIGHT (Police-constable G 105). I received charge of the prisoner, and produce the articles, which were given to me by Mr. Freeman - the prisoner said her little boy took them, and she was not conscious of it.
Prisoner's Defence. The little boy put them in unknown to me.
NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18320517-219]
1346. JOSEPH HENLEY was indicted for stealing, on the 13th of April, 2 pairs of callipers, value 3s.; 1 hammer, value 3s.; 1 fuller, value 1s. 6d., and 1 chisel, value 1s., the goods of James Wilmott.
JAMES WILMOTT. I live in Hind-street, Hatton-wall. I deal in tools, and am a smith - the prisoner was in my father's employ, and afterwards in mine for two years; he had my tools to work with, but was not to take them away- I missed two pairs of brass callipers, and some other tools; I spoke to the prisoner about them - he said another person, named Hudson, had taken them, whom I had employed two days in the week; I afterwards went to a house in St. Luke's, with an officer - I found the prisoner there, and these callipers in a tea-caddy, and under the bed I found this hammer, and this fuller - he said they were his; I can speak to these two pairs of callipers with certainty, as being mine; one of them is broken at one end, and the other overlaps.
Prisoner. I brought them up to town with me when his father sent for me - I worked for him seven years, and then for his widow, when this prosecutor turned her out of the house; and now he wants to get me out - he has reduced me to four days' work in a week; I used to live in the house. Witness. I had a spare room, and I let him have it - he said, when those things were found, that he brought them from the country; I cannot say any thing about the hammer and fuller, but I lost such things.
HENRY HUDSON. I work for the prosecutor. There were two pairs of callipers there, and this pair I can be certain of; I have known this these twelve years - this hammer I am certain of, the prisoner made it himself.
JOHN LEAVER. I work for the prosecutor. I have known this hammer for eighteen months, and this fuller I can swear to by some marks on the neck of it - it is the prosecutor's; the prisoner knows that he made another a size larger, because this was too small for some work.
Prisoner. About four years ago this man was discharged for stealing a hammer, and I got him in. Witness. I was discharged, but I never knew what for.
SAMUEL FREEMAN. I have worked for the prosecutor, and have known the prisoner twelve months. A few weeks ago he came to do some work at my place; he brought some tools, and said they were for his use; he threw them down, and said he would b-y soon alter them; this gave me suspicion, and I told the prosecutor; the prisoner came one morning just as I opened my shop, and took the tools.
THOMAS MORRIS (Police-constable G 27). I took the prisoner at a house in Little Mitchel-street - I found these articles there; he said they were his own.
The prisoner put in a written Defence, stating that the tools were his own - and that he had gone into business on his own account, which had caused this prosecution against him.
JAMES WILMOTT. I believe he had gone into business.
NOT GUILTY.
Fourth Middlesex Jury, before Mr. Sergeant Arabin.

These boots belong to Mrs.Kerenhappuch Armstrong - I was serving a customer, and saw the prisoner going from the shop, putting something under his coat - I looked over my board to see if he had taken a pair of boots which were there, but he had not; I pursued, but he got away; the officer afterwards brought him and these two boots, which I know are Mrs. Armstrong's - I am quite sure he is the man I saw going from the shop; I knew nothing of him before - I have the two fellow boots to them.
Prisoner. Q. Did you see me in the shop? A. Yes, you were retreating from the shop - you came begging, and took them.
JOHN NEWTON (Police-constable E 32). I was on duty near the King's-cross, and saw the prisoner come running towards me - I stopped him, and asked what he had got; he said a pair of boots, which he was going to take to St. Giles'; I found those two odd boots - I went to Islington, and found the prosecutor.
Prisoner's Defence. A man I knew gave me them to mind for him.

SMITH JOBSON TAYLOR (Police-constable C 111). On the night of the 20th of April, the prisoner came to me in Regent-street, and desired me to take him into custody; he said he had committed a robbery some days before, and taken three saws from Nassau-street - I went there, and found it was true; the next morning I went to the prosecutor, who gave me the duplicates.
JOHN GEORGE TEMPLE. I am in the service of a pawnbroker; I took in one saw from the prisoner, I believe - and here is another I did not take in.(Property produced and sworn to.)
SMITH JOBSON TAYLOR. I have a certificate of the prisoner's former conviction, which I got from Mr. Clark's office.
JOSEPH SKINNER, JUN. They were my saws and tools, which he took at that time.
Prisoner. You well know the tools I took at that time were not stolen; they laid on the bench week after week. Witness. They were for use, but not to be pawned.
GUILTY. Aged 40. - Transported for Seven Years.

1356. FRANCIS SMITH was indicted for stealing, on the 16th of May, 1 spoon, value 1s. 6d., the goods of James William Henry Faulkner.
MARY FAULKNER. I am the wife of James William Henry Faulkner, a surgeon, at Pimlico. The prisoner came there on the 16th of May, for one ounce of salts, and 2d. worth of assafoetida; I served him and he went away; I went up into the drawing-room; I then saw him come twice to the door and go away - he came a third time and then knocked; I went to him; he said he wanted double the quantity of each article; I said I could serve him with the salts, but I did not know where the other was - while I was doing that he said I must write a direction for him; there then came a knock at the door; he asked if he should answer; I said if he pleased; he went, and I heard him say No; he came in and said it was a young man who wanted to know if any cabinet-maker lived there; my husband then came in, and said the other young man had taken his coat from the hall - I said, "Take this one into custody," but the prisoner got out; I did not miss anything then, but the officer brought him back in about ten minutes, with this spoon.
Cross-examined by MR. LEE. Q. When had you seen this spoon? A. Not two minutes before, in the parlour cupboard; he went looking round while I was serving him.
JOHN BARKER (Police-constable B 153). I saw the prisoner running about a quarter of a mile from the house; I suspected all was not right - I took him; he took out his handkerchief, and I saw him clasping something in his hand; I asked what it was, he said "What is that to you?" I found this salt spoon in his hand broken.(Property produced and sworn to.)
GUILTY. Aged 22.

On the night of the 5th of May I was outside serving, and saw the two prisoners together in the shop - there was a pile of pieces of pork; Rumbold looked over it, and I saw Metcalf draw a piece from underneath, but it being slippery it fell on the ground - the man was at this time covering her; I went in, and picked the pork up - the woman coloured very much; I gave it to my fellow-shopman, and said, "John, put this away, it is dirty;" the prisoners then went to the back of the counter, and took a piece of bacon - they put that down, went to the cheese counter, and stood by some ladies, who were being served - they then came to the butter counter, and asked for - lb. of 8d. butter; I then saw Rumbold take this knuckle of ham, and give it to Metcalf, who put it under her apron - they paid for the butter, and were going out; I saw the officer, and called him; Metcalf dropped the ham from under her apron.
JOHN PERKINS. I was serving in the shop. I saw the prisoners come to the back of the shop, and take a pieceof bacon - I had my eyes on them; they put it down, and went to the butter counter - I did not see the ham taken, but saw Metcalf drop it.
JOHN BORRODELL (Police-constable H 136). I took the prisoners, and have the ham.(Property produced and sworn to.)

1361. WILLIAM HURLEY was indicted for stealing, on the 28th of April, 1 watch, value 15s., the goods of William Gray.
RUTH GRAY. I am the wife of William Gray - he is a baker. On the 28th of April the prisoner came for some flour for his master; I had no one at home, and I sent him to the bakehouse to get a pie out of the oven, which he did- I then sent him to fetch some coals - a watch, which had usually hung in the bakehouse, was missed the next morning.
SARAH NASH. I am single - I have known the prisoner about five weeks. On the 28th of April I met him in the New-road, between nine and ten o'clock; he asked me to take a watch to pawn for him, which I took to the Edgware-road, and pawned it in the name of William Roberts, as he told me - I did not know his name.
HENRY MATTHEW BAYFIELD. I am a pawnbroker, and live in Lisson-grove. I took in this watch on the 30th of April, but not from the witness.
SARAH NASH. I pawned it at another place - it was taken out the second morning, and brought to me; I cannot swear whether this is the watch - it is something like it; I did not notice it - I cannot say whether I think it is - I do not exactly think it is the watch.
Prisoner's Defence. Mrs. Gray sent me for the pie and some coals - there was a boy in the bakehouse.
JURY to MRS. GRAY. Q. Was there any other person in the bakehouse? A. The man who works there came to me at six o'clock, and I paid him - he said all was right, but he is not here.
NOT GUILTY.
