OLD COURT. WEDNESDAY, MAY 23.
First Middlesex Jury, before Mr. Recorder.
1370. JAMES BRENNAN was indicted for feloniously forging a bill of exchange, which is as follows:-March 29, 1832.
Three days after date pay William Well, or order, the sum of 20l. which place to account of JAMES ALDERSOM.
Messrs. Thomas Westfield, Carlos, and Co., No, 5, Pope's Head-alley, Cornhill.
with intent to defraud William Venables and James Walford.
SECOND COUNT, for uttering the same.
MR. BODKIN conducted the prosecution.
RICHARD VENABLES. My brother William, and another, carry on business as haberdashers and linendrapers, in Lamb's Conduit-street - I assist in the shop. On Tuesday, the 3rd of April, a person came and asked for cash for a bill; it was after the gas was lighted - between five and eight o'clock; I cannot be more particular as to the time - I was in the middle of the shop, No. 75, when he came in; we have more than one shop - I noticed his person, and am positive it was the prisoner; he presented this bill to me, and asked for change for it - I told him it was not customary to change bills for parties we were not acquainted with; he said his name was Boot, that he lived at No. 41, Guildford-street, and was well known in the neighbourhood - his appearance being respectable, and knowing that persons living in that street were respectable, I desired the boy Olney to give him the amount for the bill, and saw him do so - I saw him give him a pen, and saw the prisoner writing on the bill, but was not near enough to see what he wrote; he wished me good night as he went out - the bill was presented next day, and discovered to be forged; on the following Saturday I met the prisoner in Lansdowne-place, by the side of the Foundling - he was round the corner, nearest to Guildford-street (our shop is two doors from Theobald's-road); he was going towards Russell-square - he turned round, and looked at me; I immediately crossed, and overtook him at the corner of Brunswick-square - I asked if he would oblige me with his name and residence; he refused - I do not exactly recollect his words; I then took hold of his arm, and said I wished him to walk with me to Navarino House, Lamb's Conduit-street, immediately, respecting a bill which I had; he said he lived over the water, and wished me to go with him to a friend of his, in Henrietta-street, which is in the neighbourhood of Brunswick-square; I refused - he then told me if I did not loose him he would have a knock at me; he immediately struck me; a scuffle ensued; I eventuallygave him in charge of a Policeman - I called at No. 41, Guildford-street, and found no Mr. Boot living there; Mr. Lucas has lived there many years.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q.Do you know that the prisoner's defence is an alibi? A. I have been told so - I did not fix the time before I knew that; I can only say the gas was lighted, and it was dark; I had never seen him before - we sell almost every thing at our shop; I was not at that time aware that a bill of this nature should have a stamp - I never saw one like it before - the name of Westfield and Co. is printed; I did not particularly examine it that night - I know this happened after twilight.
MR. BODKIN. Q.Which occupied your attention most, the bill, or the party presenting it? A. The party.
HENRY BENT. I am apprentice to the prosecutors. On Tuesday, the 3rd of April, I was behind the counter when this bill was brought - Mr. Venables stood nearly opposite me, with his face towards the door; I am certain the prisoner is the person who presented something to Mr. Venables - it was talked of as a bill; I only saw a paper in his hand, which seemed rather dirty - I heard him ask for change; Mr. Venables said it was not customary to do so; I heard nothing else - Mr. Venables sent him up to the desk, and Olney asked him if he would have notes or sovereigns; I saw the prisoner write something on the opposite counter to where I was, and give the paper he wrote on to Olney, who delivered some sovereigns to him; he was in the shop nearly five minutes - there was a gas-light just over where he stood; I saw the prisoner again at Hatton-garden on the Saturday following, and knew him directly I saw him - I cannot say at what time he came, it was after we had our tea; we generally finish tea about a quarter past five or six o'clock - it appeared to be quite dark out of doors; our shop closes between eight and nine - we have no fixed time; I cannot say when we closed that night - I think the prisoner came after six and before nine.
Cross-examined. Q.Will you swear it was after six o'clock? A. I cannot be certain, but should think so - I never heard Mr. Venables say at what time it was; I never heard him say it was half-past four or five o'clock - the prisoner's witnesses were examined at the office; Mrs. Courtney and her husband, and others - I cannot say whether they were sworn.
MR. BODKIN. Q.Was there more than one examination? A. Yes, three - Mr. Venables was examined the first time; I cannot say whether the prisoner's witnesses were examined at the second or third.
DANIEL SUTTON OLNEY. I am cashier to Messrs. Venables. On the 3rd of April, by Mr. Venables' desire, I gave twenty sovereigns for this bill to the prisoner, who wrote his name and address on the back of it, on the counter, in my sight - I did not notice him so as to know him again.
Cross-examined. Q. Perhaps you can give us some notion as to what hour it was? A. I think it was past six o'clock - I had come from tea about half an hour, and generally finish tea about six.
THOMAS BARFORD. I am a Policeman. On Saturday, the 7th of April. I received the prisoner in charge; Mr. Venables was examined at the first and last examination - there were not three; Mr. Venables was examined on the Saturday, the prisoner was remanded till the Saturday following, but nothing was done on account of Mr. Phillips' absence - he was remanded till Monday; there was no examination then, and he was remanded till Thursday the 19th, and then committed; on the day the prisoner was apprehended, he wrote his name and address in my book, here it is (reads) - " J. Brennan, No. 6, North-terrace, Marsh-gate, Lambeth;" Mr. Laing asked a gentleman some questions on the prisoner's behalf at the first examination.
Cross-examined. Q. Who was that gentleman? A. A person from Henrietta-street; Mr. Venables fixed the time at the first examination at between five o'clock and eight; I never heard him fix a nearer time - I think five or six witnesses were examined for the prisoner; I cannot say whether they were sworn - I think they were; Mr. Laing ordered the prisoner to be admitted to bail, in 500l. himself, and four sureties of 250l. - he was not bailed.
MR. BODKIN. Q.How many times was he brought up for examination? A.Only twice.
ROBERT HESKETH PHIPPS. I live at No. 5, Pope's Head-alley, Cornhill, and have occupied the whole house for seven or eight years - no such persons as Westfield, Carlos, and Co. did business there; I never heard of such a firm, and never saw bills of this kind before.
Prisoner's Defence. I have had no opportunity of explaining the circumstance; the Magistrate behaved very harsh to me, and public reports have gone forth as if I was guilty of the crime; the inconsistency of the nature of the bill must he evident to any gentleman concerned in trade; on the day I was arrested, I was going to Henrietta-street, Brunswick-square, to bring my friend to the Adelphi theatre, for which I had orders, and was going into Brunswick-square, when I felt a touch on my shoulder, and on turning round, I saw a person who was a perfectstranger to me; he requested my name - I asked his object, and when he persevered, I said it was Brennan, and that I lived over the water, thinking he would then perceive his mistake - he says he met me, but he could not, he was behind me; on his stopping me we had a scuffle - I told him to loose my arm; he said he would not - I refused to go with him, not knowing where he would take me; at the examination, as far as I recollect, he named the time to be five o'clock, or thereabouts - I never saw the bill, and my witnesses will prove my occupation during the whole day.
MARY COURTNEY. I am the wife of James Courtney; we live at No. 6, North-terrace, Marsh-gate, Lambeth. I have known the prisoner seven years; he is a landscape-painter, and came over here the latter end of January, at my husband's invitation - he boarded at our house, but did not sleep there; his painting-room was in our house - he has held situations of trust. I remember all the transactions of Tuesday, the 3rd of April; he breakfasted with us that morning, about ten o'clock - he was engaged, finishing a landscape; he went to his painting-room after breakfast- we dined about five o'clock; (the prisoner, my husband, and myself, in the back parlour.) and after dinner removed to the front parlour - my husband is a reporter and attends the Houses of Parliament; his hour of attendance that week was seven o'clock - the prisoner was in our company from five o'clock till my husband went to his duty; I have two children - I did not miss the prisoner from the fire for ten minutes from dinner time till half-past ten at night; I did not miss him at all, but he could not be away ten minutes without my knowing it; there was alittle coolness at that time between my sister-in-law and Mr. Courtney - she came to the house that evening about half-past eight or nine o'clock, and the prisoner saw her part of the way home, at half-past ten o'clock - she lived with us, but was going to a friend of hers, who was in trouble; Needham, a mason, called at our house about seven o'clock that night, and waited about an hour for Mr. Courtney's return - Mr. Courtney went out again between nine and ten o'clock, and did not return till twelve; I am sure the prisoner did not go out.
MR. BODKIN. Q. You have known the prisoner seven years - have you been in Ireland part of that time? A. I have been more than three years in London, but my husband corresponded with him - we are not related. On the 3rd of April the prisoner was very ill - my sister-in-law being out all day, and my servant having left, I was obliged to do every thing myself, and I made soup for him; my servant had left two months before, and my sister-in-law came to stop with me; I have a servant now - she came about three weeks ago; my sister-in-law had been out all that day, and all the night before, which made the day remarkable to me, having so much to do; and I had been ill and was weak then; my husband was learning drawing of the prisoner - I was examined at Hatton-garden at the last examination; my husband and the prisoner drank gin-and-water after dinner, but before dinner they were drawing.
MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Was what you said at the office taken down? A. If it was it was done quickly; I think my husband went out when our clock struck seven, and we keep it ten minutes too fast, on purpose, to give him warning; I was out of the room at times for five minutes; the prisoner was lounging on the sofa, and reading something - I have seen the prisoner write, and believe the paper produced to be written by him; I was asked about that at the office, and said I did not think it was his, but it was then in the clerk's desk, and I only saw the J.B., but when I took it in my hand, and looked at it, I said it was his.
MRS. BYRNE. I am Mr. Courtney's sister. On Monday, the 2nd of April, I went to see a friend, whose husband had been drowned on the Sunday, and staid all night with her; I returned to my brother's about twenty minutes past seven o'clock on Tuesday evening, and on going into the garden in front of the house, and looking in at the window, I saw the prisoner sitting there; I saw nobody else, but he appeared in the act of speaking, and thinking it might be my brother, I was afraid to rap, as there was a coolness between us; I did not go in - I returned in about ten minutes; I still saw the prisoner in the act of talking, and returned up the terrace again; I returned to the house about half-past eight o'clock, and then went into the kitchen - I afterwards heard the door shut, and went into the parlour; my brother had just gone - nobody but the prisoner was there; he was on the sofa, and did not appear in good health; I remained there till half-past ten o'clock, and he then left with me; I went to Mrs. Torren's, New-street, St. James' - he came with me as far as Charing-cross.
MR. BODKIN. Q. Did you live with Mr. Courtney? A. Yes, since about a week after Christmas; the servant had just gone - I assisted Mrs. Courtney; there are short venetian blinds to the parlour window - they were folded too, but not turned; I swear the prisoner was there.
NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18320517-244]
1371. ISABELLA SWAN, JAMES DAVIS, and HENRY JOHNSON were indicted for feloniously assaulting George Garness, on the 27th of April, putting him in fear, and taking from his person, and against his will, 1 watch, value 1l.; 1 chain, value 6d.; 1 seal, value 2s.; 2 keys, value 1s.; 1 purse, value 1d.; 1 half-sovereign, 2 half-crowns, and 1s., his property.
GEORGE GARNESS. I live in Lamb's-passage, Chiswell-street; and am a servant at Mr. Gosbell's stables, On Friday night, the 27th of April, between eleven and twelve o'clock, this happened - I had been drinking ale with Phillips; we had two pints at the St. Paul's, in Chiswell-street - I drank one pint, and felt myself a little merry, but knew what I was about; I was going home, and Phillips asked me to have a pint of beer - we went to the Golden Boot, Milton-street, about ten minutes past eleven o'clock; Swan sat about three persons from me in the tap-room, under the window - I did not notice the other prisoners - I sat there for about five or ten minutes; they were singing, and called on me for a song; after some persuasion, I attempted to sing, but most of the company made such a howling, I left off, and Phillips got up, and said, "George, let us come out;" I was in the act of following him, when two men pulled me back by my jacket, and pulled me on a seat in the middle of the tap-room; they pressed me to sing again, and I began; Phillips came to the door just after I had left off the second song - (as they made the same noise,) Phillips said I was wanted, that master wanted the keys of the stable; I said I was coming, and got up - I was pulled back by the same two men; Phillips came into the room in a short time, and caught hold of my collar, to bring me out - we had got about the middle of the tap-room, when the gas-light was put out; I had not noticed any of the prisoners but Swan there - I do not know where she was at that time; when the light was put out, I felt Phillips loose his hold of my collar, and I received a blow on my legs - I was tripped up - I fell on my back on the floor; the tap-room door being directly opened, I saw a man's hand; he had a great coat on - I saw he had got my watch-chain, which he broke from my watch; the watch was then hanging to my guard, out of my fob; Swan took it off the guard; I could see her by the light from the bar, as the tap-room door was open, and I was close to it- there were several women in the house; I did not know Swan before - I caught hold of Swan's clothes, by which means I was able to raise myself up - I had made a snatch at the watch as she took it from the guard - it fell from her hand on the ground, and was kicked from there; I do not by whom - Swan picked it up, and put into her bosom; I stood by the tap-room door; Swan was close by me - I called to Atkinson, the landlord, "I have been robbed of my watch;" I did not tell him who had taken it, because there was such a scuffle - I was so exhausted, I could hardly speak; I had hold of Swan at that time; I let go of her as some people came huddling by me - I was pushed away from her, and at that moment Phillips came to me with my purse, and said, "Here is yourmoney, I took it from that man," pointing to Johnson, who was within hearing - Swan was making towards the front door, which leads into the street; the Policeman was coming in at the time - Mrs. Atkinson, the landlady, said to him, "Stop that woman! she has got the watch;" he stopped her - she drew nearer to the counter with the Policeman; I saw her draw the watch from her bosom, and give it to him, saying, "There is the watch" - the Policeman kept it; the chain, seal, and keys were entirely gone from it - I did not see Davis in the house at all, but as the Policeman was taking Swan to the station, Davis came and said he should not take her, for she was his wife; Gladwell took Johnson, and another took Davis.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q.You had your senses about you? A.Perfectly so - I sang very little; I do not recollect seeing Davis in the house.
Cross-examined by MR. CLARKSON. Q.You went to one or two public-houses? A. To the St. Paul's public-house; I am sure I had no spirits - they wanted me to drink some gin; I refused, and they spilt it down my neck - I do not know who that was; I knocked the glass away and none went down my throat - I gave the same evidence before the Magistrate, and did not want Phillips to remind me of any thing; I once thought of leaving this prosecution alone, as I was afraid of losing my situation - I never heard Phillips tell the Policeman he should have 3s. 6d. a day; he never said so in my hearing.
Q. Were not you so drunk that Swan picked up your watch, and gave it to Phillips to save for you? A. No, she did not - the landlord and landlady saw her take it from her bosom when the Policeman came in; I did not want more drink there, which was refused - when the tap-room door was pushed open I could see by the light from the bar.
Cross-examined by MR. BARRY. Q. Had you been sparring with Phillips? A.No; there were a great many people in the tap-room - I do not know at what time I lost my purse; I bottoned my breeches pocket up a moment before Phillips came in - that was before the two men pulled me back; when I saw Johnson he stood facing the door - I cannot swear he was in the room when my watch was taken - I did not see him till Phillips brought my purse, and pointed to him; it could not have fallen from my pocket - Peak, the Policeman, did not tell me to say the prisoners were the persons, or they would get off - it was Phillips gave Johnson in charge.
HENRY PHILLIPS. I am cook at an eating-house in Chiswell-street. I went with Garness to the Boot, and had a pint of ale - I had passed through the St. Paul's public-house when he was there, but I drank nothing; we both went into the Golden Boot between eleven and twelve o'clock, and had a pint of beer between us at the bar, and as there was singing in the tap-room, Garness said, "Let us go in, and hear a song, and drink the rest there" - we went in and sat down ten or eleven minutes; they called on Garness for a song - I saw Swan and Davis in the room at that time; Garness attempted to sing, but they howled, and he left off - I got up and said, "We had better be going;" he said, "Stop a bit" - I did not see him follow me; I was not out half a minute, and when I came in again he had shifted his seaf, and was sitting between two or three men, who called on him to sing again.
Q. Had you seen any thing when you said he had better be going? A. I saw some persons rubbing their hands across his pockets, and thought all was not right - I went out, came in again, and, to get him out, said,"George, your master wants the keys of the stable;" he got up, and attempted to come out - they pulled him down, and said, "Let him finish his song;" that was neither of the prisoners - they began to sing again; I asked if he was coming - he attempted to get up, and was pulled down again by the same persons - I went over to the house opposite, to get somebody to pull him out; I was not gone half a minute - I returned, and saw him sitting quietly in the same company as before; I laid hold of his collar to pull him out, and the gas was immediately put out - I heard somebody in the room say ding; I was struck, and a bad expression used to me - I was separated from him; I ran to the tap-room door, and opened it - that threw a light into the room; I saw Swan round him and Johnson, but did not see Davis - I saw Swan drop the watch; she then kicked it into the bar; I saw Johnson take the purse out of Garness' pocket - I snatched it from his hand; Garness was then on his back on the tap-room floor; I saw Johnson's hand come from the pocket with the purse-Swan and others were making their way out of the room, and the landlady said, "Stop that woman, she has got his watch in her bosom;" I got hold of it, and the Policeman took it out of my hand - Davis came up, and said she was his wife, and should not go to the watch-house - he was in the house; the Policeman took her to the station - there was an attempt to rescue Swan, and by that means they were all secured.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. It was nearly all over before Davis interfered? A. He was in the room all the time, but I only saw him attempt the rescue - I was sober.
Cross-examined by MR. CLARKSON. Q.Was the prosecutor sober? A.He was half and half - he knew what he was about; he had no gin at the Boot; I have left my situation because I was not satisfied with my food; I have not talked with the Policeman about how much a day I should get here.
Cross-examined by MR. BARRY. Q. You made no charge against Johnson at the station? A. No; I do not know whether he was locked up for an assault - the robbery was committed in the middle of the tap-room, right opposite the door.
GEORGE ATKINSON. I am landlord of the Golden Boot. I was inside my bar the early part of the evening - the three prisoners came in about eight o'clock, in company with two men, and about eleven Garness and Phillips came in - they had some beer in the tap-room; they had no spirits, to my knowledge; after they went into the tap-room I heard the prosecutor singing - he was sitting under the window; I had not heard singing before - I observed some of my customers near him, but not either of the prisoners were at that time near him; I then returned to the bar, and was standing there; Johnson came out with two women, and called for some gin - I do not recollect whether Swan was one of them or not; while Johnson was drinking at the bar a Policeman came in, and said,"Take in a light;" I took a candle into the tap-room, and found the gas-light out - I lighted it directly, andsaw the prosecutor laying on the floor - Phillips had hold of his collar; I stopped there a little while, then came out, and heard somebody say, "This is the woman who has got the watch" (I had heard the prosecutor say nothing); I did not see what woman was meant, and who said so I do not know; I saw the Policeman come in, and he took the female prisoner by the side of the bar - I did not see the watch taken from her, nor see it at all.
Cross-examined by MR. CLARKSON. Q.Did you see Swan standing at the bar with Davis' wife? A. A young woman was drinking there with Davis; Swan was in and out that evening - two women and two men were at the bar at the time the light was called for, but whether one was Swan I cannot say; the bar is on the same side as the tap-room; when the gas was out I do not think a person could see what was passing, till I took the candle - a great many people were running in and out; I am sure nobody could see, for I am in the habit of going in when the gas is out - it was as I came out from lighting the gas that somebody said the woman had the watch - I did not see it kicked by anybody, but after they were taken to the station-house I saw the remains of the watch-glass close to where the prosecutor lay; when I heard my wife say"She has got the watch," I saw it open in Swan's hand, not in her bosom - the Policeman ran in at that moment; she was standing in front of the bar, not attempting to go out; Garness was so drunk, that at the station-house he could not give a word of his statement; he sat in a chair there, and shammed a fit - Phillips was not drunk; two strange bad characters had come into my house, and at the time of the robbery they ran out.
Cross-examined by MR. BARRY. Q.Are you sure Johnson came out of the tap-room with two women before you heard the noise? A.They were outside at the time the robbery was committed, and I left Johnson standing at the bar when I went, in and found Garness on the floor - I did not see Phillips take the purse from Johnson, nor hear it mentioned either at the house or station - it was about half-past eleven o'clock.
COURT. Q. Do you keep your house open so late? We are allowed to keep open till twelve o'clock all round the neighbourhood.
- ATKINSON. I am the landlady. The first thing I saw was the prisoners standing at my bar - Garness ran out, put his back to the door, and said, "Oh, I am robbed, I have lost my master's watch;" at that time Johnson and Swan were at the bar - Johnson had just called for a quartern of gin; Swan stood with her right arm at the corner of the bar - I saw her stoop down, and pick up a watch with her right hand; I never saw it in her bosom - she picked it up between the bar and the tap-room door - the prosecutor was so tipsy he could not stand; he reeled from the door, and the Policeman came in; I said,"Here is the woman who has got the watch;" the Policeman took her round to the right-hand side of the bar, and she delivered the watch to him - she had it open in her hand before that - neither Garness nor Phillips said any thing about her taking it at that time; when Garness saw it in her hand he charged her with it - when the Policeman came; he could scarcely stand then.
Cross-examined by MR. CLARKSON. Q.Did you tell any body to stop Swan? A. I did not - I said, "Policeman, here is the woman who has the watch;" she was not attempting to go away - she could have no hand in stealing the watch.
MATTHEW PEAK. I am a Policeman. I was going by the house on my beat, and heard singing - I looked through the window, and saw Phillips go towards Garness; they sat right opposite the tap-room door - he said something, but I could not hear what; after he had been there about a moment the gas was put out or went out - I then put my head in, and told Atkinson to take a light in; he was in the bar at the time - there were men and women standing at the bar, but I cannot say who; in three or four minutes I heard Garness say he was robbed of his watch - he was then in front of the bar, and had come out of the tap-room- he had been drinking, but was not so drunk but what he knew what he was about - as soon as I heard he had lost his watch I entered the house, and Swan was then coming out of the street door - I had not seen her at the bar; Mrs. Atkinson said, "Stop that woman, she has got the watch; she has got it in her bosom" - I went to get hold of her; she put her right hand into her bosom, and took the watch out - it had no chain or seal to it then; she was not standing at the bar - I am quite sure Mrs. Atkinson said she had it in her bosom; and I saw her take it from her bosom - Mrs. Atkinson was in the bar at that time, but I think Mr. Atkinson was in the tap-room; as Swan took the watch from her bosom Phillips held his hand out to get hold of it, but I took it from him - after I got the watch I put Swan into the passage, and sent for assistance, because I had been ill-used before by taking persons out of that house; the landlord sent a boy to the station for assistance, and a man came and kept the people back while I took her out - Davis claimed her as his wife in the public-house - Atkinson and his wife were there; Davis took hold of her gown, and said, "Come away, are not you coming home?" he said she was his wife - he pulled me by the sleeve, but used no violence there; when I had got Swan twenty or thirty yards from the house, Johnson came behind and hit me on the back of the head; I had heard no charge against him then - I called for a Policeman, and Gladwell took him - Garness came to the watch-house; he said the watch did not belong to him, and raved about it, so that as last he went into a fit - before that he was sensible, and knew what he was doing - when Johnson struck me he sprang the rattle for me.
Cross-examined by MR. CLARKSON. Q.Have you heard Mrs. Atkinson's evidence? A. I have - on my oath she told me to stop Swan; Garness came to me after the parties were fully committed, and said, "Some of their friends have been to me, what am I to do? "I said, "You are bound over to appear, and may do as you like;" he said something about being afraid that he should lose his place in consequence of the time it would take - the public-houses are open till twelve o'clock; we have no orders to prevent it - Mrs. Atkinson's name was struck from the back of the indictment, by the clerk, at the time we were sworn, as she was gone away ill in a coach; she was present when the bill went before the Grand Jury, but was not called -I did not know what she would state; she contradicted me before the Magistrate, respecting Swan's coming out of the door.
Cross-examined by MR. BARRY. Q.How long wereyou in the house? A. I dare say ten minutes altogether; when I got a certificate from the Magistrate, Phillips asked why I had two papers; I said one was a certificate for 3s. 6d. a day - he did not tell me he had seen Johnson's hands come from the prosecutor's pocket, and that he took the purse from him, till next morning.
GEORGE GLADWELL. I am a Policeman. I was in Chiswell-street, and met a boy coming to the station - he sent me to the Boot, where I found Peak, who asked me to keep the people away while he got Swan out; when he a few steps from the door, he called out - I went up, and saw Johnson strike him once or twice on the back of the head - I secured him; Davis was on the spot, and I received several blows from somebody.
FREDERICK MILES. I am a Policeman. Atkinson's servant fetched me from the station; I found Johnson in Gladwell's custody - Davis said I should not take him away, as he was his brother, and I took him for attempting to rescue Johnson; Atkinson and his brother forfeited their recognizances yesterday against another prisoner.
Swan's Defence. What the prosecutor has sworn is false - it is not in my nature to do any thing of the kind.
Johnson's Defence. I came out of the tap-room to go home, and during that time two men whom I knew came in, and we had something to drink at the bar; while it was being poured out the other two prisoners came out to the bar, and partook of what I was drinking - a man named Sadler came out; he had been singing - he stood there talking: a scuffle eusued in the tap-room, but I never moved from the bar - a watch was kicked out of the tap-room; the female prisoner took it up - I never offered to go from the bar till she was taken out; I then went to see where she was going - the Policeman then collared and dragged me away; the prosecutor was very much intoxicated - the Policeman said I was taken for an assault; they said nothing about the purse. In the morning, at the office, the prosecutor said to the Policeman, "Is that the woman?" he touched him with his elbow, and said, "Yes, and these are the two men - you must mind what you say, or they will get off," and then they charged us all with the robbery.
CHARLES NUTT. I live in Holywell-lane. I went into the Boot at ten o'clock on the night in question, and saw the prosecutor, who I have known for years, sitting between two young men, singing; he told me he had been to sea - he was a little intoxicated: I told him his master wanted to speak to him outside, as he had the key of the stable, thinking if I got him out he might be induced to go home - Phillips was not in the room then; several songs were sang - I saw him drink three glasses of gin, which two young men gave him, and several times he drank out of different pots of porter; I then tried to prevail on him to go home, but he said, "Mind your own business," and that he would cut my head with a quart pot; he called for more gin - one of the men who sat on his right, called for a quartern, and poured out a glass - he was so tipsy he could scarcely take it; the prisoners were not those men when I went in the male prisoners were at the further end of the room, at some distance from him; the young men put the gin to his mouth; he drank some, and some he spilt down his clothes - he then began to sing another song- there was no room for me to sit; I stood at the tap-room door, and had another pint of half-and-half; Swan and Johnson asked a young man who had sung to come and have something to drink at the bar; nothing had been done to the prosecutor at that time, for I stood opposite him; Swan and Johnson left the room, and stood - I stood aside, opened the door, and let them out with two more young men; when they had been out about five minutes, three young men came into the tap-room, and took the seats they had left, by the fire-place, and in a few minutes the gas was put out - I thought it was done accidentally; I opened the door, and told Atkinson they were in the dark - he ran in with a light; I did not go into the room again, but sat on a table opposite the bar; when Atkinson took in the light I saw the prosecutor laying on the ground - Swan was then at the bar, drinking spirits, and Johnson had the measure in his hand, close to her; there was another young man with them - the tap-room was nearly full - as soon as the door was opened people rushed out into the street, and I saw the watch kicked out of the taproom; Swan stooped and took it up as she stood at the bar- it is impossible that she could have interfered in the robbery; she had left the tap-room for a quarter of an hour -Mrs. Atkinson said, "This woman has got the watch;" she was not going away - Atkinson did not say Stop her! the watch was open in her hand - the prosecutor said, "Give me the watch, and I will run away, for I will never give any body in charge;" a Policeman coming in saw her with the watch in her hand, and took her in charge by the bar; she never had the watch in her bosom, on my oath; it was in her hand, and partly in the prosecutor's hand - she was in the act of giving it to him when the Policeman came in; I was holding him at the time, for he could scarcely stand.
COURT. Q.What are you? A. A house-painter - I believe the prosecutor had his senses so as to know what he was saying - any body could hear what he said; I cannot say whether Mrs. Atkinson heard it - I was close to her, but she was the only person to take care of the bar - she might not have heard it; she said, "That woman has the watch," but that was not to the Policeman - it was before he came in; she said it to the prosecutor - I cannot say she did not say it again when the Policeman was there- I do not think she did.
NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18320517-245]
1372. FRANCIS HENRY PEMELLER was indicted for stealing, on the 4th of May, 15 sovereigns, 8 half-crowns, 15 shillings, and 10 sixpences, the monies of Richard Marshall and another, his employers. - To which he pleaded GUILTY.

[trialAccount t18320517-246]
1373. JANE LOVELL and REBECCA WILD were indicted for stealing, on the 11th of April, 13 yards of ribbon, value 7s., the goods of Thomas Hall.
JAMES EYNON. I am shopman to Thomas Hall, linen-draper, Bishopsgate. On the 11th of April, between three and four o'clock, the prisoners came into the shop together, and asked for some bonnet ribbon, and I served them; Lovell took several ribbons out of the drawer and unrolled them - she attempted to take one piece, but lost it the firsttime, and at last she took it; Wild at last bought two yards, which she paid for - they went out; I went after them, brought them back, and Lovell dropped the ribbon in the shop.
Cross-examined by MR. WALESBY. Q.Can you recollect how many customers you had in the shop? A. Only one while they were there, to the best of my belief -Lovell took the ribbons out of the drawer, and gave them to the other prisoner, after unfolding them, and she put them into the drawer again, unfolded, so that the drawer was in confusion; Lovell reached over the drawer, covering it with her shawl, and took a ribbon, but caught my eye, and dropped it - she made a second attempt, and took it; I think she put it in her breast - I saw it drop from her when I brought them back.
JAMES BLEECHMORE. I am an officer. I took them into custody; I took a basket from Lovell, and found in it another piece of ribbon, claimed by Mr. Rotherham.
(Property produced and sworn to.)
Lovell's Defence. I bought both pieces of ribbon in Rosemary-lane, of a young woman with carrotty hair, for 5s. 6d. - she said she would not sell one without the other; as I returned I met Wild, who asked me to go with her to buy some ribbon; I showed her mine, she did not like it -I went with her to a shop in Houndsditch; she bought two yards; I said I did not like it, and put her out of conceit of it, and she asked me to go into this shop to buy another.
LOVELL - GUILTY. Aged 20.
WILD - GUILTY. Aged 19.
Transported for Seven Years.
There was another indictment against the prisoners.
[trialAccount t18320517-247]
1374. RICHARD HOOD was indicted for stealing, on the 28th of April, 1 handkerchief, value 3d., the goods of George Bown, from his person.
GEORGE BOWN. I am a musician. On the 28th of April, about ten o'clock, I was in Fore-street - I did not feel my handkerchief taken, but missed it as I came out of a public-house; the prisoner was taken in five minutes.
THOMAS CADE. I saw the prisoner and a little boy standing at the top of Milton-street - they went behind the prosecutor, and I saw the little boy take his hand from the prosecutor's pocket; they walked away up Fore-street - the prisoner was standing behind the little one; I am sure of his person - the boy ran away when he saw the prisoner was taken.
THOMAS NORWOOD. I was in Fore-street, and saw a boy take a handkerchief out of the prosecutor's pocket and give it to the prisoner, who stood just behind him; he was secured, and the handkerchief found.
WILLIAM BRAND. I am a marshalsman. I heard a witness say the prisoner had gone up Milton-street - I followed, and took him and the boy, but in scuffling with the prisoner the other party let the boy go; I found the handkerchief in the prisoner's pocket.(Property produced and sworn to.)
Prisoner. I beg for mercy.
GUILTY. Aged 26. - Transported for Seven Years.

[trialAccount t18320517-249]
1376. JOSEPH JENN was indicted for feloniously uttering a forged receipt for goods, with intent to defraud Isaac Bird and another.
ISAAC BIRD. I am in partnership with Robert Johnson; we carry on business as ironmongers and hardwaremen in Fenchurch-street. The prisoner was in our employ for about eleven months, and left on the 23rd of April - this receipt came into my hands, and was posted in my books as a genuine receipt; the prisoner was entrusted to take orders and receive money - I do not know Mr. Tutt's handwriting; the prisoner was in our employ at the date of the receipt.
Cross-examined by MR. BARRY. Q. Where did you get that paper? A.From a drawer in our counting-house, which our warehouseman and clerk have access to; it is an open drawer - I have only one customer named Tutt; his Christian name is John - there is no address in the receipt; I will swear the receipt is in the prisoner's hand-writing.
Q. If he had not sold the goods to Tutt, this would be the readiest way to lead to a discovery? A. Yes, he generally writes his orders in his memorandum book, but this is a receipt; I should think the signature was not the prisoner's writing, but the body of it is.
ROBERT JOHNSON. I am in partnership with Mr. Bird. Tutt is a customer of ours; part of the goods named in this receipt were delivered to us from Birmingham, on the 17th of February - the prisoner had told me before that he had an order for chisels from Tutt, of Wilstead-street; on the morning of the 18th, when he came, I told him the chisels had come, and he had better take them out with him - I afterwards found they were gone from the shelves; they must have been taken by the prisoner or our warehouseman - on the same evening the prisoner brought me this receipt, which it was his duty to do, if he had delivered the goods; he gave it me as the genuine receipt of Tutt - I put it into the drawer, or gave it to Mr. Bird - something having occurred afterwards respecting the prisoner's conduct, I delivered accounts to our customers - Tutt pointed out these articles as never having been ordered or received; I produced to him different receipts - he said this was forged.
Cross-examined. Q.When goods were heavy, who took them out? A.Our porter; if more than 120l. worth of town business was done in a month with retail shops, the prisoner was to have a quarter per cent; he had 25s. a week certain; that sum has never been exceeded, and we owed him no commission - we never allowed him to purchase goods with our money - I never knew him take goods to customers, who had not ordered them, when intended for another customer who did not like them.
JOHN TUTT. I am a smith. I never ordered these goods; I never received them, nor signed this receipt - I do not sell these sort of goods - (read).
Received from Bird and Johnson, the 18th of February, 1832, 3 sugar cleavers, 1 dozen of chisels, 3 ladies' hammers, half a dozen plyers, and 2 pairs of brass candlesticks. J. TUTT.
GUILTY. Aged 44. - Transported for Fourteen Years.
There were two other indictments against the prisoner for embezzlement, and the prosecutors stated their loss at 320l.
[trialAccount t18320517-250]
1377. CHARLOTTE HOLLAND was indicted for a misdemeanor.
MESSRS. PRENDERGAST and BARRY conducted the prosecution.
THOMAS WATSON. I am overseer of St. Mary, Aldermanbury. I commenced giving the prisoner parochial relief on the 6th of April, 1831, and continued to do so till the 28th of March, when I went out of office - I paid her 3s. a week at first, and increased it on the 30th of November to 4s.; I have paid her altogether 10l. 8s., believing her to be chargeable on our parish - she presented herself as a pauper of that parish at the vestry, weekly - her name was in the book which was handed to me by my predecessor.
Cross-examined by MR. ROWE. Q. How are the entries made in that book? A.Weekly - the casual poor are entered in a different account; she made no representation to me, except coming with the other paupers, and taking the weekly allowance.
MR. BARRY. Q. Was she entered as a weekly pauper before you came into office? A. Yes, and I find, in 1830, an instrument was bought for her to go to work with.
BENJAMIN BROWN. I am one of the parish officers of St. Mary, the Virgin, Aldermanbury - I came into office on the 4th of April. The prisoner applied to me that day for her weekly allowance; I paid her 6s.; Mr. Watson had given me the list, and had reduced her allowance to 3s. a week, which she refused to take, saying she had a great deal of illness in her family, and I gave her 6s.
Cross-examined. Q. You know nothing of her settlement? A. No.
JOSEPH WEST. I am master of the workhouse of St. James', Clerkenwell. I have paid the prisoner 5s. a week from the 3rd of June 1829, to the 14th of March 1832.
JAMES HILL. I am clerk to the vestry-clerk of Clerkenwell. I took and examination of the prisoner's husband, and at his death, of herself - she claimed a settlement on her husband's apprenticeship in 1829.
Cross-examined. Q. Was there ever any question about her settlement? A.Never; she said her husband died at sea.
GUILTY. Aged 25.
Recommended to Mercy. - Confined Three Months.
[trialAccount t18320517-251]
NEW COURT, WEDNESDAY, MAY 23.
Fifth Middlesex Jury, before Mr. Common Sergeant.
1378. THOMAS BAKER and HENRY McGEE were indicted for stealing, on the 1st of May, 1 necklace, value 8s., the goods of Charles Barnes, from Maria Barnes; and that Henry McGee had been before convicted of felony.
MARY BARNES. I am the wife of Charles Barnes; my daughter Maria is eight years old; I put a necklace on her neck on the 1st of May; I afterwards saw her without it.
MARIA BARNES. I am the daughter of Charles Barnes. On the 1st of May I went out with Mrs. Lathan, to look at the chimney-sweepers; I had a necklace on -Baker snatched it off my neck, and gave it to the other prisoner, who ran away towards the market; I laid hold of Baker's coat, and some persons came to my assistance.
JOHN MEARS. I am the son of William Mears. I was in Bath-street on the 1st of May, looking at the sweepers; I saw Baker snatch the necklace off Maria Barnes ' neck; he turned his hand round, and gave it to the other prisoner; they both ran through the mob, but Baker was stopped; Maria Barnes said, "You thief, give me my necklace;" he said, "I have not seen it;" I said, "Yes, you have, I saw you."
McGee. I was in the country at the time; this witness's word is not to be taken - he is nothing but a bad character. Witness. I am sure it was you; I know you perfectly well.
HENRY BERESFORD (Police-serjeant G 8). I was at the station when Baker was brought in; I found nothing on him - the other prisoner was described, and I took him a week afterwards.
THOMAS HOBBS KING(Police-constable N 248). I produce a certificate from Mr. Clark's office of the former conviction of McGee; I was present, and know he is the person - he was convicted by the name of McGinn, and whipped.
BAKER - GUILTY. Aged 14.
Whipped and Discharged.
McGEE - GUILTY. Aged 18.
Transported for Fourteen Years.
[trialAccount t18320517-252]
1379. WILLIAM TAMWELL MILLER was indicted for stealing, on the 7th of April, 3 yards of carpet, value 5s.; 1 chaise-cushion, value 3s.; 1 blanket, value 2s.; 3 half-blankets, value 4s.; 2 quilts, value,.; 4 sheets, value, 10s.; 1 pillow, value 4s., and 1 pillow case, value 1s., the goods of Joseph Parker.
JOSEPH PARKER. I live in Ann-street, Globe-fields, and am a painter. I was in the country; I returned, in consequence of a letter from my wife - I found my house stripped, the children laying on a mattress, and all the furniture gone; it was represented by my wife that it was by a distress warrant; she gave me this paper - I went to the landlord, and have no reason to believe there was any authority for what was done; the prisoner called on the Tuesday morning following, and appointed to meet me at the landlord's house at eleven o'clock, previous to the goods being condemned - he failed in his appointment; there was no address on the inventory which he had left, and the landlord could not tell me where to go to him; I found him on the following morning at the sale-room - I had made an arrangement with the landlord on the Wednesday morning, and procured from him a written order for the delivery of my goods; I tendered it to the prisoner who refused, to give them up and said he should sell them - I attended the sale, and took an account of what each lot sold for - they fetched 5l. 6s.; the goods I charge him with taking, were not included in the inventory he left, but some of them I believe were sold; my rent was 2l. 8s, and was two or three days over due.
DAVID EDWARDS. I am landlord of the premises. Parker owed me 2l. 8s., for rent, which ought to have been paid on the 1st of April; I authorized the prisoner to put a distress into the premises on the 5th of April, and signed his warrant, which is here.
JOSEPH PARKER. I know the articles I charge the prisoner with taking were in his possession; I saw themat the sale-room; they were lotted - some of them were sold but others were not.
Cross-examined by MR. DONNE. Q.Were you residing in the house? A.I was in the country; I saw the goods at Mr. Bird's sale-room, in Whitechapel-road; when I presented the order to the prisoner, he refused to deliver them, and said they had been condemned the day before, for 2l. 7s. 6d.; there were all my goods there except a watercock and another article; he offered me the goods back for the 2l. 7s. 6d. on the Wednesday morning - I said Mr. Edwards had agreed with me to compromise the matter and showed him Mr. Edwards' order, but what he offered me were the goods in the inventory only.
COURT. Q.What did he say to that? A. He said he did not know what I meant by three half-blankets; he said there was an old flannel-gown - I then mentioned the chaise-cushion - he said he had done perfectly right, and it was of no use my bouncing him; he turned his back to me; I mentioned the whole of the articles which are in the indictment, and he gave me no further information -I then asked him for a copy of the condemned inventory; he pulled out a book, from which he ran over some items so quick I could not detect the articles I was in want of; I said I should expect an account of them; he said he should not give it me then, but would in proper time - he passed me in the street two or three days afterwards, and sent a man across the street with an account to me, but the articles I was in want of are not in it; it does not agree with the inventory, as it gives no enumeration of the goods at all - I do not think I could re-place all my property for less than 10l.; it sold for 5l. 6s., among brokers at the sale-room - I took down what the lots sold for.
Cross-examined. Q. Did you take it down? A. Yes, on this catalogue; I bought one or two lots, as we were in immediate want of them - the prisoner offered me the goods in the inventory if I would bring him 2l. 7s. 6d.; but the others he said he knew nothing about - I afterwards went to the office, and the Magistrate granted me a summons on a charge of taking away goods and not giving me an account, and likewise of not paying me over the surplus.
LYDIA ELIZABETH PARKER. I am the prosecutor's wife. The prisoner took away besides what are put down in the inventory, two quilts, a blanket, three half-blankets, a flannel dressing-gown, a chaise-cushion, a brass water-cock, and about three yards of carpet.
JOSEPH PARKER. I saw the chaise-cushion at the saleroom; it was not sold; I saw it there a week afterwards - the goods were openly displayed in the auction-room.
Cross-examined. Q.Who sold the goods? A.Mr. Bird; I did not see any money given to the prisoner; I did not apply to the auctioneer for the goods; I saw them at the time of the sale - but they were not in the sale-room this morning.
COURT. Q. As there is no address on the inventory he left at your house, how did you know where to look for your goods? A. He has written on the bottom of it that the goods were impounded at Mr. Bird's - I had known the prisoner by sight before; this inventory does not mention the things stated in the indictment, but I believe they were all at the sale-room - I am not aware that any were omitted though they were not all sold: I believe it is regular for the auctioneer to receive the money, and hand it to the broker, who pays the landlord.
DAVID EDWARDS re-examined. Q. What sum of money did you receive of the prisoner? A.This is the account he gave me (read) - "Goods condemned, 2l. 7s. 6d.; expences, 17s. 8d. - balance, 1l. 9s. 10d.;" this was before the sale - he gave me to understand that Mr. Wilmot took the goods at the condemned price; he did not render me an accountv of the proceeds of the sale; I was not at the sale - the prosecutor had the offer of the goods at the condemned price; I did not know that any goods were taken which were not in the inventory - I offered that if he brought me a sovereign and left me a few things, he might have the remainder.
Cross-examined. Q. Did you see the inventory? A. Yes, and it was with reference to that that I offered him to restore his goods.
MR. DONNE to JOSEPH PARKER. Q. Was Mr. Wilmot present when you were offered the goods back? A. I believe he was, but he was not close - the rooms are large; Mr. Wilmot denied that he had purchased the goods, but not in the prisoner's presence - the prisoner had admitted to me that he did not buy them, and at the sale he offered me part of the things back for what money I had in my pocket, at a price he chose to put upon them.
COURT. Q. Then he assumed to sell to you? A. Yes, my Lord, and he said that he had sold some of them, but I was at the sale to prove he had not sold them.
MRS. PARKER re-examined. Q. How long before the prisoner came and made this inventory had you seen your things all safe? A. On the same day - I am sure I saw all the things named in the indictment; I told the prisoner my husband was out of town, and begged him to wait - he came in on the Thursday morning, and took the goods about tea time on Saturday; I went to the landlord - he said he could have nothing to do with it; it was in his broker's hand - I could not look at the inventory when he left, as my children were screaming - he left nothing but a mattress and a blanket.
Prisoner's Defence. There might be an article or two which were omitted to be put down, but every article was placed at Mr. Bird's - I never went near them, nor touched them, till I went with Mr. Wilmot, and he condemned them- if you look at the inventory the prosecutor produces, you will see every article inserted - the cushion he speaks of, is regularly lotted as sundries, and sold with some other articles; I never received a single farthing, but the 2l. 7s. 6d. of Mr. Wilmot, from which I deducted the expences - I was taken to Lambeth-street, and the Magistrate dismissed the case; I then heard that there was a bill found against me, and I gave myself up.
THOMAS DUFFIELD. I am a broker's porter, and live in North-street, Whitechapel-road. I was employed by the prisoner to remove the goods from Mr. Parker's, about the 6th or 7th of April - I took them in a cart to Mr. Bird's sale-room; Anderson and I took the articles, which the prisoner told us to take - I did not see the prisoner take any thing himself; I had been in possession of the goods.
COURT. Q. Did you or the other man move any thing, but what the prisoner told you? A. No; we left behind a mattress, a blanket, and several small articles of sundries- I received a chaise-cushion from Anderson, and gave it to a man named Pole, who was loading the cart.
WILLIAM ANDERSON. I assisted Duffield in removing the goods from Parker's premises - there might be some persons about, but not many.
COURT. Q. Do you remember giving a chaise-cushion to Duffield? A. I do not recollect seeing it - I delivered nothing but what the prisoner told me.
ROBERT BOONE. I am warehouseman at Bird's auction-room. I received the goods from these witnesses - I kept them till the day of condemnation; I was present when they were condemned by Mr. Wilmot, a broker - I saw an inventory in their hands; there were several people present, but I cannot say who - it took place in the second room; I looked out the lots, and Mr. Wilmot went round and put his price upon them; they then said they would go and look for Mr. Parker - I think this was on a Monday or Tuesday early in April; I saw Parker at the room the next day, with the prisoner.
COURT. Q.Upon your oath, on whose account were the goods sold? A. That I cannot tell - I attended the sale, but did not look at the book; I do not know who had the money - I swear I never heard from the prisoner; there was a constable, but he did not come into the room - Mr. Wilmot's goods have been sold, and I believe he is gone to America.
Prisoner. I had no other appraiser but Mr. Wilmot - we went to some constable in Mile-end; I do not know his name.
NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18320517-253]
1380. WILLIAM BUTTERWORTH was indicted for stealing, on the 13th of May, 1 handkerchief, value 4s., the goods of Henry Taylor, from his person.
MARGARET TAYLOR. I am the wife of Henry Taylor, a cheesemonger, who lives in Union-street, Bishopsgate. I was returning home with him on the 13th of May - I felt something; I turned, and saw the prisoner, who had nearly got my husband's handkerchief out of his pocket; I seized him - he threw it behind him; I took it up, and gave him in charge.

Prisoner. I was pushed up against the lady by two boys - they took the handkerchief, and threw it down.
The prisoner received a good character from his master, who engaged to employ him.

1383. THOMAS JONES was indicted for stealing, on the 5th of May, 1 brass gas bracket, value 10s., the goods of Charles John Hunt, and fixed to a certain building of his; against the Statute.
WILLIAM RICE. I was a Police-constable. On the afternoon of the 6th of May I saw the prisoner and two others, under the Quadrant, in Regent-street; I suspected them, and told the officer - we watched them for about twenty minutes, and then saw the prisoner coming out of a house with a brass bracket, which he dropped at the door - I secured him.
JOHN BROWN. I am servant to Charles John Hunt, who keeps a billiard-room, in the Quadrant. I was on the staircase at one o'clock on the 5th of May - I heard a noise, and saw the prisoner in the passage; he opened the door, and ran out; I saw this bracket at the door, on the ground - Rice brought him back.
JAMES KENNERLEY (Police-constable C 30). I was in Regent-street, and watched the prisoner and two others; I saw the prisoner enter the house, and I saw him brought back by Rice - I found this nut of the brass pipe in his pocket; he seemed in great distress.
GUILTY. Aged 21. - Confined One Month.

1384. JAMES KENT was indicted for stealing, on the 23rd of April, 1 coat, value 10s.; 1 waistcoat, value 2s.; 1 pair of trousers, value 10s., and 1 sheet, value 3s., the goods of Thomas Kent.
THOMAS KENT. I live in Satchwell's-rents, Bethnal-green - the prisoner is my son. On Easter Monday I went to work early in the morning; the prisoner came to me afterwards; I gave him leave to go home, and do some work - he took the key of my apartments; I returned a little after eleven o'clock in the forenoon; I found the key in the door - I went in, and missed a suit of clothes, which had been in a box in my room; I saw him again on the Thursday afterwards; I got an officer, and he was taken - I found a duplicate on the mantelpiece in the room.
ELIZABETH AGAR. I am the wife of Thomas Agar - we live in Cottage-court; I can see the steps of the prosecutor's door from our house. On Easter Monday I was standing on the steps of our door, and saw the prisoner come down the steps of his father's door with a bundle; he turned to the right, towards Church-street.
PHILIP COOK. I was in Church-street, Bethnal-green, on Easter Monday. I met the prisoner (who lives within three doors of me) going with a bundle towards Mr. Cassles, the pawnbroker.
HENRY HUDDY (Police-constable H 72). I took the prisoner on his father's premises.
GUILTY. Aged 14. - Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18320517-258]
1385. JAMES MARCH was indicted for stealing, on the 29th of March, 1 iron pot, value 7s.; 1 saucepan, value 2s.; 1 petticoat, value 1s., and 1 sheet, value 6d., the goods of William Sanders.
SARAH SANDERS. I am the wife of William Sanders, and live at Enfield. On the evening of the 29th of March, I placed a tin saucepan, with a flannel petticoat and sheet, outside my door, in the yard; they were missing, as well as the pot, the next morning.
Cross-examined by MR. DONNE. Q.When did you see them last. A.About seven o'clock in the evening.
JAMES WALDER. On the evening of the 29th of March I saw the prisoner at the prosecutor's yard, about half-past eight o'clock or near nine; he asked if I had seen his aunt - I said No; he said he had seen her once, and she told him if he came again, he should have his trousers - I went to the Holly Bush to get half a pint of beer, and as I came back, I saw him coming from their yard, with a bundle; he saw me coming, and dropped it; he said the kettle was so b-y hot, he could hardly tie it up in the petticoat - he went in with a stick, but came out without it; he dropped the bundle in the garden.
Cross-examined. Q. What time was this? A.Near nine o'clock, it was quite dark - I had known him before.
SARAH SANDERS. I found a stick in the yard - this is it; he had not been to me that evening - he is my nephew.
Witnesses for the Defence.
JAMES BIRD. The prisoner was in my employ on the 29th of March; he met me at five o'clock in the morning at Enfield, and came to London with me; I sent him with my cart to the Queen's Head, the watering-house, in the Green-lanes, and to wait there for me; my brother persuaded him to go on - I was detained in London, but I overtook him with the cart, about two miles and a half before he got to Enfield; he left me at Chase-green, at half-past eight o'clock, which is about a mile from the prosecutor's; I gave him some money - he came to me the next morning, and said his aunt had been accusing him of stealing these things; that he had gone to the stable with the horse and things, and then to bed directly at Mr. Leifchild's, the auctioneer.
WILLIAM SANDERS. He told me the next morning that he did not sleep at Enfield, but at Botany Bay - I have never seen the things since.
COURT. Q.Had you had any quarrel with the prisoner? A. No; he had formerly worked for me, and he might do so again, for his victuals.
WILLIAM SMITH. I am a labourer, and live at Enfield. I have known the prisoner from an infant; about half-past eleven o'clock that night, I was coming along the road, and heard somebody stepping in the road, or in Mr. Sander's premises; I stopped at the side of the road, and saw a man come out there, with a pot of some kind - he brought it across the road, and took the lid off; he held it up, and poured something out of it, which made a rattle - I made up to him - he put on the lid, jumped over the bedge, and away he run; I saw some potatoes in the road.
SARAH SANDERS re-examined. Q.Were there any potatoes in the pot? A. My husband had been boiling potatoes in it, and put it outside with the potatoes in it.
NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18320517-259]
1386. ANN PHILLIPS was indicted for stealing, on the 15th of April, 1 pewter pot, value 1s. 3d., the goods of James Clayton, and 1 pewter pot, value 10d., the goods of Thomas Goodman.
JOHN POSFORD (Police-constable S 50). On the night of the 15th of April I was on duty in Pancras-road. I went after the prisoner, in consequence of what a gentleman told me; I saw a man with her; I stopped the man first - I found nothing on him but 1s. 1 1/2d.; I then followed the prisoner, and just as I got up to her, I saw her put her hand to the top of some pales, and drop these two pots over - I stopped her, and took the pots up; she denied doing it - the man got away.
JAMES CLAYTON. I keep the Poulterers' Arms, in Seymour-street . This quart pot is mine - I live three-quarters of a mile from where the prisoner was taken; I give 1s. 6d. a piece for these pots.
THOMAS GOODMAN. I keep the Euston Castle ; this pint pot is mine - the prisoner was taken three hundred yards from my house.
Prisoner's Defence. The Policeman overtook me on road, and asked what I had thrown over there - I said, Nothing - he said, "Stop, till I go and get them;" I never saw them.
GUILTY. Aged 33. - Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18320517-260]
1387. JOHN SMITH was indicted for stealing, on the 2nd of May, 1 watch, value 3l., the goods of John Leeson.
SUSANNAH LEESON. I am the wife of John Leeson. -On the morning of the 2nd of May the prisoner came to my house, to inquire after me - he had formerly lodged in the same court, and had shown some attention to my brother-in-law when he was in the hospital; I went down stairs to buy a bundle of wood, and left the prisoner alone in the room - on my return I met him on the stairs; I had not been gone above three minutes - I said, "Are you going?" he said he would return in a moment - on my going up I missed a watch, which I had left hanging over my mantelpiece - when I went down it wanted twenty-five minutes to nine o'clock; I had not been out of the house for the wood - I ran after him; he was running down an alley into Rose-lane, and then he took some of the back streets, and turned into Wentworth-street; I have never seen the watch since, and the prisoner never returned.
Prisoner. She went down the court, and said, "Are you going?" and I said "Yes, but I shan't be long before I come back," but I met a man, who detained me. Witness. I had not left the house.
CHRISTOPHER SANDERS. I am the witness' brother-in-law. I had seen the prisoner at their house once before; I met him, and told him my brother-in-law wanted to speak to him - he said, "I am going there;" I said, "Well then, I'll go with you;" we went on till we came to the top of the Minories, and then he ran away, in a different direction - I ran and took him; I got him to Somerset-street, but no farther - I then got an officer, and gave him in charge.
Prisoner. I was not two yards from him from the time he first spoke to me. Witness. He set off to run, but I stopped him immediately.
MARY SANDERS. I was with my husband when he stopped the prisoner at the corner of the Poultry, and said"My brother-in-law wants to speak to you;" he said,"I am going there;" I said, "My brother-in-law only wishes you to give up the duplicate, if you have it; I know he will forgive you;" he went to the Minories, and then tried to run off; my husband seized him, and would not let him go.
Prisoner's Defence. I never saw the watch that morning; I had seen it there before.
MRS. LEESON. I take my oath it was there.
GUILTY. Aged 33. - Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18320517-261]
1388. GEORGE WOOD was indicted for stealing, on the 15th of May, 1 bridle, value 20s., the goods of Henry Vansittart.
JOHN HADLOW. I am helper in Mr. Vansittart's stable, or in the Hay-mews. On the 15th of May I was in a room over the stable - I heard a noise and went down stairs, I saw the prisoner walking from the stable - I went after him, and when he saw me, he ran up the mews towards Union-street, turned to the left, and then again into Charles-street, and down Chesterfield-street; I pursued - he threw the bridle down the area of No. 8 - I pursued, and took him back; a woman picked up the bridle, and gave it me from the rails - it is my master's; I said to him, "What have you done with the bridle?" and he said, "I have not a bridle about me;" I said, "Which area have you thrown it down;" I had seen it safe twenty minutes after two o'clock, and pursued him with it about four.
JOHN FLOOD (Police-constable C 7). I was on duty in Park-lane, and received the prisoner and the bridle; he said he took it through distress, to save a family from starving, and begged me to let him go - in going through the street, he begged me not to expose him publicly; I took him through some back streets, and he ran off - he beckoned the people to make way for him, and he told them to stop me - I got very ill used.
GUILTY. Aged 31. - Confined One Month.
[trialAccount t18320517-262]
1389. JOHN WILSON was indicted for stealing, on the 11th of May, 1 watch, value 13s., and 1 jacket, value 5s., the goods of Alexander Baird.
WILLIAM SCOTT(Police-constable K 186). I received a watch from Wolfe Harris - I took the prisoner on the 11th of May: I told him I apprehended him for stealing a watch and a jacket of Alexander Baird 's; he said, "I know I stole them."
WILLIAM DAVIS (Police-constable K 254.) I took a jacket from the prisoner when he was apprehended; he said he took it, and put it on that morning - the watch he said he had sold to get a breakfast.
WOLFE HARRIS. I am a general-dealer, and live in Rosemary-lane. The prisoner came to my shop with another person on the 10th of May - he produced this watch, which I gave 10s. for; I saw the prisoner with Baird the same evening - Baird said it was his watch, that he gave 23s. for it in Dublin many years ago; the prisoner said he took it.
Prisoner's Defence. The prosecutor and I went out and got very tipsy; there were several other sailor s in the house - we had a bed a piece; we threw our clothes in the middle of the room - we had to go out early in the morning, and he, or one of the other sailors, put the watch into my pocket instead of his.
GUILTY. Aged 27. - Fined 1s. and Discharged.

1391. JOSEPH WYATT was indicted for stealing, on the 14th of May, 1 pair of trousers, value 19s., and 1 handkerchief, value 1s., the goods of Joseph Careys.
JOSEPH CAREYS. I lodge in Britannia-street, and the prisoner lodged in the same room. On the 14th of May I went out and left him in bed - I returned between ten and eleven o'clock; I missed my trousers and handkerchief -I found him at his mother's, in Cross-street, the next morning.
MARY PARKER. I am a widow, and live in Britannia-row. I went with the prosecutor and a Policeman to the prisoner's mother's house - the prisoner produced a pair of trousers to the officer; he had not left my lodging.
CHARLES KNOTT (Police-constable N 11). I went to the house in Cross-street with the prosecutor on the 15th of May - I found the prisoner on a temporary bed, under the counter in his mother's shop; I asked where the trousers were - he at first denied knowing any thing about them, but he at last gave them to me; I took him to the station, and found the duplicate of the handkerchief on him.
FREDERICK GILES. I have the handkerchief, which was pawned by the prisoner, on the 14th of May.
The prisoner received an excellent character, and a person engaged to get him on board a ship.
GUILTY. Aged 21. - Confined Ten Days.

1392. JOHN SHIELDS was indicted for stealing, on the 14th of May, 1 bridle-bit, value 10s., the goods of Robert Lambert.
EDWARD MORRIS. I am foreman to Mr. Robert Lambert; he is a coach-proprietor, and lives at Blackwall. On the 14th of May I missed a bridle bit, which I had seen safe the afternoon before - I accused the prisoner of taking it; the officer produced it to me.
WILLIAM DICKENSON (Police-constable H 11.) I produce this bit, which I got from Mr. Beacham.
LEWIS BEACHAM. I live with my father, who keeps a sadler's shop in Whitechapel-road. The prisoner came on the 15th, and offered this bit for sale for 1s. 3d. - my father asked if it was his own; he said it was, and he offered him 1s. for it, which he took.(Property produced and sworn to.)
The prisoner received a good character, and his master engaged to take him again.
