1393. JOSEPH PINDER and WILLIAM DEAR were indicted for stealing, on the 18th of May, 3 shirts, value 12s., the goods of Thomas McDougal.
THOMAS McDOUGAL. I am a school-master, and live at Minerva-terrace, Islington. On the 18th of May I was in the lobby of my house; I received information, and went into my garden with a candle, where I had a quantity of linen hanging to dry - I saw the mark of footsteps in the mould - I missed some shirts; I had a boarder in the house - I am responsible for his linen.
JOHN JOHNSON (Police-consable N 41). I was on a piece of waste ground, behind the Haberdashers' Alms-houses, on the 18th of May; I saw the prisoner going from that ground to the East-road, at a quarter-past ten o'clock in the morning - it is two or three miles from the prosecutor's; I caught hold of Dear as he was going past a cart, and I got a person to stop Pinder, who had got round the horse - I asked Pinder what he had in his bundle (each of them had a bundle); he said he was going to the mangle for his mother - his bundle appeared wet; I asked Dearwhat he had - he made no answer; I took them to the station - I found this table-cloth in Dear's bundle, and these three shirts in Pinder's; Pinder was asked, before they were opened, what he had, and he said three shirts, one of his own, and two of his father's, and they were not marked.
(Property produced and sworn to.)
Pinder's Defence. We were minding them for a baked potatoe boy, who said he would not be five minutes.
PINDER - GUILTY. Aged 12.
DEAR - GUILTY. Aged 14.
Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18320517-267]
1394. FRANCIS PEARCE, JOSEPH GRIFFITH, WILLIAM FENNELL and JOHN DAVIS were indicted for stealing, on the 12th of April, 160 lbs. of lead, value 2l., the goods of John Powell Powell, and fixed to a building of his; against the Statute, &c.
MR. BODKIN conducted the prosecution.
CHARLES DEARE. I am a solicitor. I know the premises, at Fulham, from whence this lead was taken; Pearce's (the prisoner) brother had been tenant of them, and had quitted on the day before this transaction - I think the place was empty; the premises belong to Mr. John Powell Powell.
WILLIAM DUNN (Police-constable V 152). I was on duty at Fulham, on Thursday, the 12th of April, about ten o'clock at night; I know the house in question; it had been inhabited by Pearce's brother - I heard a noise of persons talking aloud; I went into the stable, and saw the prisoners there - Pearce was standing up, and the other three laying down: the lead I now produce was in a bag, under some straw - they did not appear to be asleep; I asked what they wanted there - Pearce said what did I want to know for, and that the premises were his; I said,"They don't belong to you now, but they did to your brother, and you must go along with me;" Clark, (who was with me), and I, took them into custody - I found this knife on Davis and another on Griffith.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Did you leave either of them behind? A. Yes, we left Davis, because he bid himself; the other three used violent language, and said they would have my life, but they afterwards went willingly, when my brother officer came in - I swear it was in the stable I saw them, it is not far from the shop- it does belong to the house; I know Pearce's brother had lived there a few days before - I found no lead on the prisoners - they were laying on it; we did not take the lead the first time - one of them threw a brick at me.
JAMES CLARK. (Police-constable V 138). I went in, and found this officer and three if not four of the prisoners were round him, making use of violent language to him - I cannot say there were four; I am certain there were three - the officer said he had a charge against them; I took two of them into custody - I afterwards went, and found Davis in the brick work of the forge; he made a noise sufficient to prove he was asleep - the place was narrow, and we had to drag him out by the heels.
Cross-examined. Q. What interval elapsed before your return? A. I should think from half to three-quarters of an hour - Davis had that opportunity of absconding.
DANIEL ALLSOP. I am a plumber, and live at Fulham. I fitted this lead to the middle gutter in the roof of Mr. Powell's house, at Fulham; it appeared to me to correspond with the place, and what was left, in all respects - it had been lately cut - there is about 1 1/2 cwt. or 1 1/2 cwt.
Cross-examined. Q. You could not say how long it had been cut? A. No, certainly - I think I had seen it on the building a month or six weeks before - one of these knives would cut lead, but not the other.
JAMES KING. I am a builder, and live at Fulham. I had been over this building about a month previous to the removal of the lead; it had been taken off a shop where it could not be observed.
MR. ALLSOP. It was taken from the roof of a smith's-shop - there was no difficulty to get up; it joined the house - the whole of the premises had been occupied; the stable was detached.
MR. DEARE. I do not know myself who was in possession of the premises on the 12th of April - they had been taken possession of under a distress-warrant.
PEARCE - GUILTY. Aged 25.
GRIFFITH - GUILTY. Aged 23.
FENNELL - GUILTY. Aged 23.
DAVIS - GUILTY. Aged 25.
Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18320517-268]
Fourth Middlesex Jury, before Mr. Common Sergeant.
1395. MARY NEALE was indicted for stealing 8lbs. of bacon, value 4s., the goods of Thomas Allwright.
THOMAS ALLWRIGHT. I am a cheesemonger, and live in Holborn. On the 18th of May the prisoner came into my shop, and asked the price of a piece of bacon - I was in the parlour; I turned, and saw her going out - I went, turned up her shawl, and found this piece of bacon; I asked what she meant by taking it - she said she was very sorry, and hoped I would not prosecute her; I did not know her - here are 8lbs. or 9 lbs. of bacon.
CHARLES MOORE. I am errand-boy to the prosecutor. I saw the prisoner enter the shop, and take the bacon, which she put under her shawl; she was walking out, and my master asked what she wanted - she came back, offered 5 1/2. for one piece, and this piece was found on her.
ABRAHAM LUCAS SCOTT (City Police-constable 59). I took the prisoner in charge.
(Property produced and sworn to.)
GUILTY - Aged 32. Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18320517-269]
1396. JAMES MALONEY was indicted for stealing, on the 19th of May, 1 hat, value 4s., the goods of George Day, from his person.
GEORGE DAY. I live in Gray's Inn-lane - my father is a cow-keeper. On the 19th of May, at a quarter after twelve o'clock at night, I was going through West-street; the prisoner and another young man came up to me; I had a smock-frock on - the prisoner knocked me down; my mouth bled terribly - the prisoner then took my hat off, put another hat on me, and went away: I met him again near Saffron-hill; I saw him go into Black Buy-alley; I saw he had my hat on his head - I said, "I know you:" he struck me again, and I fell against a shutter; I had had no quarrel with him - when he first saw me he said,"There is a yokle," meaning a countryman.
MICHAEL RYAN. I am a watchman. About a quarter before two o'clock on Saturday morning the prosecutor came and asked if I was a watchman - I said Yes; hesaid, "I wish you had been here an hour ago;" I soon afterwards saw the prisoner and another young man near Black Boy-alley - they knocked the prosecutor down two or three times; he sang out Police! and I then thought it my duty to go out of the City into the County - I went and took them both, but I was forced to let the other go; I took the prisoner, and took this hat from him.
WILLIAM WELLS (Police-constable G 79). I heard the watchman spring his rattle; I came down West-street- I took this hat from the prisoner; the prosecutor said it was his, and that the prisoner had taken it off his head, and put an old one on - the prisoner said he bought it.
Prisoner's Defence. The gentleman I bought it of is here.
JOSEPH BARNETT. I live in Field-lane, at the bottom of Holborn-bridge. The prisoner bought a hat of me last Monday week; I could point it out from a hundred - this is the hat; it is a very singular one - it is a silk hat, and narrow in the crown; the shape of a man's head has brought it to its present from - we generally have a mark in them, but when we sell them we take it out; I know this hat, by the white trimming; I am positive it is the one I sold the prisoner - it is a fashionable hat; it was quite flat when I sold it, such as a coachman or a cab-driver would swear, from the heat of the body, but a beaver hat would not - I bought it of a lad who was apprentice to a man who was a bankrupt, at the corner of Plumtree-court- he made it for himself five or six weeks ago; I sold it to the prisoner for a crown - I did not know him before.
JAMES HILDRED. I live in Field-lane, next door to Mr. Barnett. I saw him sell the prisoner a hat between eleven and twelve o'clock last Monday week - I did not know the prisoner, but I happened to be present; it was sold in the lane - I believe this is the hat.
JURY to GEORGE DAY. Q. How long has your father lived in Gray's Inn-lane? A. About eight months - I live with him; I have been brought up to his business, which is the trunk business - I bought my hat this day fortnight, in Romford-market; there is no name in it but my own, which I put in at the office - I knew at the hat again when the prisoner came down the alley.
MARY DAY. I am the prosecutor's mother. This is my son's hat, I believe; he came home in this old one, and I said, "Where is your hat?" he said, "Never mind, it is all right" - I scolded him for buying this hat; he said he gave 6s. for it, and bought it to save his bettermost one - his father said he had bought the hat too large, and he might have let him gone with him.
GEORGE DAY re-examined. I was sober; I had been to Smithfield with a cow for my father - I was going back, and a young man with the prisoner came and said, "Give us something to drink," and he shook my frock; I said I had no money, it was my father's money, and the prisoner said "Stun him"
Prisoner. I was going home from an evening diversion, and the prosecutor was standing against a post - he said,"I have lost two sovereigns, and I have got to pay it tomorrow morning," and he said, "I have a bit of paper here;" he took a bit out of his hat; I took it, and put it under my feet - he struck me, and I struck him; we had five or six rounds.
GEORGE DAY. I had lost two sovereigns, but he had heard that since; I had to go next day to Guildhall about a young woman.
GUILTY. Aged 17. - Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18320517-270]
1397. PETER SHERIDAN was indicted for stealing, on the 13th of May, 1 coral necklace, value 15s., the goods of John Sulman, from the person of Harriet Sulman .
ELIZABETH ULYET. I am the daughter of Henry Ulyet, a carpenter, who lives in Crown-street, Old-street-road. On the evening of the 13th of May, I was looking out of our front room window, and saw the prisoner with another boy; he turned to Harriet Sulman, unsnapped her beads, rolled them up in his hand, and ran to the top of the street - I am sure he is the person; I got a fair look at him- he turned to see if any body was looking at him, but he did not see me.
Cross-examined by MR. ROWE. Q. What hour was this? A. A little before eight o'clock in the evening; I was at our two-pair of stairs window; the little girl lives four doors from our house - I did not see several other persons passing; I had seen the prisoner before; he had a round cap on; I did not say any thing when he took them.
COURT. Q. How soon was he taken afterwards? A. It might be five minutes.
EMMA RIGGLESWORTH. I am the daughter of Isaac Rigglesworth - I am nearly ten years of age. I was playing with Harriet Sulman, in Crown-street; the prisoner came and turned her round, unsnaped the beads from her neck, and gave them to a bigger boy, who was with him, and the prisoner ran away.
Q. Had you seen him before? A. No, I was next door; I could see what he did - I am quite sure he gave them to another boy when he got to the top of the street; I saw a gentleman ran after him; I did not make any noise - I told the gentleman, and he came out when he heard the child cry; my father came home from chapel, and I went in with him, but I saw the prisoner give the heads to the other boy before I went in.
GEORGE SIMPSON. I live in Crown-street. This witness called my attention to the prisoner; I pursued, and caught him running through Hoxton-square - I took him, and asked what he had done with the beads which he took from the child's neck - he said he knew nothing about them; I brought him back; he was identified by both the witnesses, and I gave him in charge.
Cross-examined. Q. How far do you live from the spot? A. Not above seven yards - I ran out as soon as I heard the child scream; it was about eight o'clock in the evening; there were a few boys in the square, but not running - there is a short turn from the street which I live in to the square; the beads were taken about thirty yards from the square - I saw the prisoner when he was about twenty yards from me; I did not lose sight of him.
MARY SIMPSON. I am the wife of George Simpson. I have the care of Harriet Sulman - I put the necklace on her neck about three o'clock, and saw it safe about five minutes before I heard her cry out; I ran to the door, and saw my husband run after the boy - her father's name is John Sulman.
JAMES BLACK (Police-constable S 208). I took the prisoner in charge.
GUILTY. Aged 14. - Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18320517-271]
1398. THOMAS BENNETT and ALEXANDER PAWNS were indicted for stealing, on the 15th of May, 1 handkerchief, value 3s., the goods of William Stephenson, from his person.
WILLIAM STEPHENSON. I live at Old Brentford. I attended a meeting of the Political Union at Brentford, on the 15th of May; we went to the One Tun - I had a large red silk handkerchief, spotted with white - we went from there to the Red Lion; I did not see the prisoners till I returned to the One Tun.
THOMAS DUNKLEY. I live at Old Brentford. I was at the One Tun, and saw the prosecutor; I saw Bennett take a handkerchief from his pocket, and give it to Pawns, who put it into his flap - I told the prosecutor; he said,"I have not lost it," and just at that moment he was called out to take tea, and in going along he felt for his handkerchief, and found, as he says, a book instead - I could not see what sort of handkerchief it was.
Cross-examined by MR. ROWE. Q. What time was the handkerchief taken? A. About half-past five o'clock; it was a public room; there might be sixty or seventy persons there- I took it to be a Belcher handkerchief, but I only saw the corner of it; the prosecutor did not put his hand into his pocket when I told him of it - he came back in about half an hour, and said he had lost it; I never said it would be more profitable for me to attend the Old Bailey, and get 5s. a day, than to follow my trade.
MR. ROWE to WILLIAM STEPHENSON. Q. Where did you see Dunkley? A. At the One Tun - I knew him before the gave me notice that I had lost my handkerchief -I felt outside my pocket, and felt a book; I said I had not lost it - I afterwards found it had been stolen; it was a full sized silk handkerchief - I missed it about half an hour after I was told of it.
Bennett's Defence. I went to the One Tun; I sat there till some person said that those who did not belong to the meeting, were to leave, and I got up and went away.
GEORGE TAYLOR. I live with my father, who keeps a broker's shop at Old Brentford. I have known Bennett from his infancy; I have heard Dunkley say several times, that he should like to get a good job at the Old Bailey, to get his crown a day; and it would be a d-d sight better for him than to set upon his seat.
COURT. Q. What is Dunkley? A. He is a kind of a translator of old shoes into new ones; he had made new shoes; I heard him say this two or three months back.
THOMAS DUNKLEY re-examined. I deny it; there is not a word of truth in it - I never said so.
WILLIAM STEPHENSON. I had in my pocket a book belonging to the society, and the substance of the book made me think I had not lost my handkerchief; the prisoners were taken on the Tuesday after.
NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18320517-272]
1399. BRIDGET COSGROVE was indicted for stealing, on the 1st of May, 1 gown piece, value 7s.; 1 pair of shoes, value 2s.; 1 cap, value 4s.; 1 shawl, value 13s.; 1 pocket-book, value 1s.; 1 tooth-pick, value 1s., and 2 sovereigns, the property of Mary Ann Sullivan.
MARY ANN SULLIVAN. I lodge in Moor-street, Bryanstone-square. I have known the prisoner three years; I met her in Oxford-street on the night of the 30th of April, between the hours of eleven and twelve - she said her husband and her had fallen out, and he would not let her in doors that night; I took her home, and she slept in the same bed with me - my shawl was on a line in the room, and my gown-piece on the top of a box; I had two sovereigns in my pocket - I fastened the door with a bolt; I awoke between five and six o'clock in the morning - she was gone; I got up - I missed all these articles.
Prisoner. Q. Did you ever see me in the streets of London? A. Yes, I have seen you many times; you have spoken to me, and have drank in the Yorkshire Grey.
WILLIAM TRODD (Police-constable C 84). On the 3rd or 4th of May I went to where the prisoner lodged, in Oxford-buildings; I saw her daughter, who said her mother was out, and she had been out drunk all night - I sent her daughter to tell her I wanted to speak to her; she then absconded - I saw her in the street, but she ran from me; I afterwards took her from a brother-officer, and asked if she knew any thing of this robbery; she said No, but she had been out on the spree.
Witnesses for the Defence.
ELIZA KEEFE. I am a servant out of situation - I live in White Lion-street, Soho. I went to the prisoner's house on the last Friday in April, in Oxford-buildings: I slept there four nights - I came home on the 1st of May; I did not lodge with her that night, but I saw her that night when she was going to bed - the next morning, before she was up, I gave her 2s. to get her children something to eat- I do not know the prosecutrix; the prisoner's husband is a pavior, and gone to work at Liverpool; they had no quarrel.
MARGARET DALEY. I saw the prisoner on the Monday night - she had no victuals; she has eight children, and one in arms - her husband had left her only 2s; I said if she would send one of her little girls with me I would let her have 2s. or 3s. - I took the little girls, gave her 3s., and went to see her home; I saw her and Keefe go to bed -I lodge in White Lion-street; my husband is foreman at the gas-house - I have the prisoner's duplicates, and I find that on the Monday morning, at half-past eight o'clock, she pawned her shoes for 1s. 6d.
MARY ANN SULLIVAN re-examined. Q. When was it you fell in with the prisoner, on the 30th of April or the 1st of May? A. On the 30th of April I met her in Oxford-street; I have had no quarrel with her - I had seen her once or twice in public-houses, and it being a disagreeable night, I took her in, and said I would give her part of my bed; when we got home, she said she disputed whether I was married to my husband, and I took the box in which my silver tooth-pick was in and showed her my certificate, and then she was satisfied - no one had access to my room, and I saw the articles safe when I went to bed; there are lodgers in the house, but they could not get into the room unless the bolt had been undone; I have never found any of the articles - I fell asleep about one o'clock,and did not awake till near six; I then missed the prisoner and the property - the door was closed; I do not know how she came to bring up the question about my being married.
MARGARET DALEY. I was with the prisoner when she went to Red Lion-square, and I got two of her children into the workhouse on the 6th of May.
COURT to WILLIAM TRODD. Q. Did you know of her applying for relief? A. I went to the workhouse on the Wednesday, and Mr. Turner told me that they put her out of doors, because she was so drunk; I understand her husband has sent her 15s. a week.
JURY. Q. Did you see her in the street? A. Yes, that was after she was charged with this, but she got from me in a moment - she was taken by another officer.
NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18320517-273]
1400. THOMAS BRIGGS was indicted for stealing, on the 12th of May, 1 necklace, value 18s., the goods of William Clarke, from the person of Emma Clarke .
WILLIAM CLARKE. I live in Butterland-street, East-road, City-road, and am a watchmaker. I went with my wife and children to Whitecross-street, on Saturday evening, the 12th of May; my wife went into a butcher's shop: I went on with the two children - I saw the prisoner pass me two or three times: my daughter Emma had a necklace on - I had hold of her hand, and felt her flinch; I missed the necklace from her neck, and saw the prisoner running round the corner - I went up to him, and he threw it down; I took it up, and took him to the officer - several of the bends were lost by the fall.
JOHN HAYFORD (Police-constable G 184). I took the prisoner from the prosecutor between six and seven o'clock on Saturday evening, the 12th of May; I have the beads which I received from him.
(Property produced and sworn to.)
GUILTY. Aged 14. - Transported for Seven Years.

SARAH SAPET. I am the wife of Peter Sapet, of Little Russell-street, Bloomsbury. The prisoner lodged with us from the beginning of March - on the morning of the 18th of May, I got a Police-constable, and missed these articles from her room - she said she took them from want.

1403. ANN WOOD was indicted for stealing, on the 16th of May, 4 sovereigns, and 3s., the monies of John Brown, from his person.
JOHN BROWN. I am a seaman. I came on shore a fortnight ago last Monday; I met the prisoner on the 16th of May, when I had been paid; I went home with her - she said the place belonged to her, and she put her hand into my pocket and took out my sovereigns; I asked what she did that for; she said to take care of them - I would not let her have them, but she would in spite of me; I still stopped, and a man came in and wanted some money of her; I rested still till the man knocked me down - I had given them the last 1s., I had to get some beer - he said she was his sister, or sister-in-law.
MARY CHALKLEY. I live in Hamblin-street. The prisoner lodged at my house; I remember the prosecutor coming - he went with her to the front room; they were there some time; the prisoner then went out for pork-chops; I saw some sovereigns in her hand, and said I hoped they did not belong to the man she brought into my place - Crouch, a man who had lived with the prisoner, came in - he knocked the prosecutor down, and I picked him up; he then knocked me down, and almost put my shoulder out - the prosecutor asked the prisoner for his money; she said if he wanted his money he might go elsewhere - he said he did not want to hurt her, he would be contented if she gave him one sovereign.
Prisoner. Q. You saw him give me two sovereigns out of his pocket, when I went to get the chops? A. No, I did not; the usage he had from your young man was shameful; every thing I had in my room was broken to pieces.
WILLIAM OSMAN (Police-constable K 61.) The prosecutor fetched me to the house - I found the prisoner there; he charged her with robbing him, and said the man had knocked him down; the prisoner came to the table, took up a knife, and said, "If you charge me with such a sort of thing, I will stick you, or rip you open;" she said the prosecutor gave her the money, and he denied it - I found no money on her.
Prisoner's Defence. I met the prosecutor; we went to another house, and I slept with him - the next morning he asked me to go with him and get some money.
JOHN BROWN. I slept with her the night before, but I paid her half a sovereign - she tore the pocket off my new jacket.
GUILTY. Aged 26. - Transported for Life.
[trialAccount t18320517-277]
1404. ELIZABETH SAUNDERS was indicted for stealing, on the 18th of May, 1 cap, value 5s., the goods of Thomas Redpath.
WILLIAM JONES. I am shop-boy to Thomas Redpath, of Upper Seymour-street, St. Pancras. On the afternoon of the 18th of May, I was outside his shop; the prisoner came up to me, I went round the corner, and when I came back I missed a cap - I went after her, and asked if she had bought a cap; she said No - I asked where she got this from; she said she did not know; I gave her to an officer - this is the cap, it has the ticket on it; it had been on a bed, on a bedstead outside the shop.
EDWARD LAMBERT (Police-constable S 169). I received the prisoner in custody; she said she had picked the cap up.
GUILTY. Aged 25.

1405. ANN JONES was indicted for stealing, on the 14th of April, 1 shirt, value 4s., the goods of Owen Parry; and that she had been before convicted of felony.
MARY COTTERELL. I am a warehouse-woman to Owen Parry, Quadrant, Regent-street. On the 14th of April, I took home some work; while I was there the prisoner came and asked for some Denmark sattin - Mr. Parry said he had not any, and asked her if it was to make shoes; she said Yes; he said he did not sell that sort - she then turned out of the shop; he asked me what she had got; I said I did not know; he told me to go and see - I went after her; she dropped this shirt, and said "Here is your shirt;" I took her back, and laid the shirt on the counter.
JOHN CORLEY (Police-constable C 144). I received charge of the prisoner on the 14th of April; I found on her a quantity of ribbon, a cotton handkerchief, several gilt seals, and a duplicate.

GUILTY. Aged 25. - Transported for Seven Years.

1406. JOSIAH HUGH EVANS was indicted for stealing, on the 3rd of September, 1 mare, price 4l., the property of Joseph Shurley.
JOSEPH SHURLEY. I live at Portland-town. 1408. MARY SULLIVAN was indicted for stealing, on the 9th of April, 32 sovereigns, 8 half-crowns, 15 shillings, and 8 sixpences, the monies of Patrick Keefe, in his dwelling-house.
PATRICK KEEFE. I lodge at Mr. Marriot's, Wild-court, Great Wild-street - the prisoner lodged in the same room with me and my wife. I missed this money from my box about two o'clock on the 9th of April; I had seen it safe on the Saturday fortnight before - it was in a green purse; I have three beds in my room, and let them out; my wife kept the key of the box: on unlocking it the purse and money were gone, with my marriage certificate, which was in the purse, between the silver and gold; it was all enclosed in a white silk stocking - the prisoner was taken up on the 18th; she slept in my room till then - my marriage certificate was found in her pocket, but only 1d. in money- four persons besides her slept in the room; the prisoner said the devil a farthing should I ever see again; she went out drunk on the Friday before, and was out all night, and on the Sunday night she was drunk.
ELLEN KEEFE. I kept the key of my husband's box in my pocket. On the Friday before the money was missed, I left the room to fetch something for my husband - I left the key in the box; the prisoner was sitting in the window, and there was an old woman, who was very ill, and unable to get out of bed; I was out about five minutes, and when I retuned the key was still in the box; I had seen the money safe that day - I did not examine the box till Monday: when I told the prisoner the money was missed she said nothing about it.
JOHN SUTTON. I am a Policeman. I took the prisoner on the 18th of April, and found Keefe's marriage certificate on her; Mrs. Keefe directly said, "Oh, Sullivan! you are guilty, for that is my marriage line" - the prisoner replied, "The devil of a farthing of the money will you ever see again;" she was rather forward in liquor.
PATRICK KEEFE. This is our marriage certificate.
Prisoner's Defence. They were fighting on the Monday, this man, his wife, and brother-in-law - the wife took up the box and swore in a most desperate manner she would never live with him another day; she threw the box about- he took his clothes out of the box, and said, "You may go, but shan't have the money;" he then looked, and said,"Oh! I am robbed of all my money" - next day they went to a fortune-teller, then got the Bible, and turned it on me- as to the certificate, she knew I had got it, for I picked it up with some papers which the prosecutor tore; she told me to take care of it - the woman who slept in the room is not bed-ridden.
MRS. KEEFE. I never saw the certificate loose about the room - it was between the gold and silver in the purse- the woman could not get up without help.
NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18320517-282]
NEW COURT. THURSDAY, MAY 24.
Fourth Middlesex Jury, before Mr. Common Sergeant.
1409. JOHN JOHNSON was indicted for stealing, on the 17th of May, 4 planes, value 10s., the goods of Thomas Goodwin.
THOMAS GOODWIN. I am a journeyman carpenter to Mr. Sawyer, and lodged in Page-street, Regent-street, Westminster. I left four planes safe last Thursday evening, in a shop at No, 38, Frith-street, which was under repair - they were on the bench, or on the mantelshelf, with other tools; I left them safe at half-past five o'clock, and locked the door; I went to work at six the next morning - I found the padlock drawn, and my planeswere gone - the prisoner did not then work for Mr. Sawyer; I never heard any thing amiss of him while he did.
JANE ELIZABETH FARRIS. I am single, and live at No. 27, Frith-street. On the evening of the 17th of May I was at work at the front window, and saw the prisoner at the door of No. 38, which is opposite; he was endeavouring to get the padlock off the door - I watched him, and saw him go to the end of the street, and then return to the door; he was still endeavouring to take the padlock off; he opened it about half-past eight o'clock, then went in, and shut the door after him - I informed Mr. Fenn, my landlord.
CHARLES FENN. I am a cheesemonger, and live in Frith-street. On the evening of the 17th of May, between eight and nine o'clock, in consequence of what Farris said, I went over to No. 38 with a person who was then in my shop; I found the door ajar; I stood on one side, and the other person on the other, and in a few minutes we saw the prisoner coming out of the shop, with some tools under his arm, and some before him; I said,"My friend, what do you do here? who are you? do you work for Mr. Sawyer?" he said, "Yes, I am Mr. Sawyer's man;" I sent for Mr. Sawyer and the Policeman - the prisoner managed to lay the tools down on the bench, close inside the door - the officer came, and took him.
JAMES BARRACK. I went to the door with Ferris, and what he has stated is correct; when the prisoner came to the door, and we asked what he was going to do with the tools, he shifted them before him - I said, "Where are you going?" he said, "I am going out;" I said,"Not out of here till somebody comes" - the officer took the same tools which the prisoner had.
Prisoner. Q. Did not you take two head planes to the station-house, and then say they were not them? A. I took none; the officer took two small planes at first, and then I said there were two larger planes, which he had in his hand - I went back, and pointed them out to the officer.
GREGORY COLE DUDLEY (Police-constable C 6). I produce the plane, which I got at No. 38, Frith-street - these two bead planes were first pointed out, and I took them; the gentleman said there were two other planes as well; I went back, and they pointed out these.
(Property produced and sworn to.)
Prisoner's Defence. I had been out of work a long time, and knowing that Mr. Sawyer would give me work, I went there, and saw the door open; I went in, and thought I should see Goodwin - I had nothing in my possession.
GUILTY. Aged 29. - Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18320517-283]
1410. JOHN GOODGE was indicted for stealing, on the 19th of April, 1 gown, value 5s., the goods of John Matthews and another.
THOMAS WILLIAM BLUETT. I am shopman to John Matthews and Leonard Clare Matthews; they are pawnbrokers. The prisoners has been a customer there; he came on the 19th of April - I was standing behind the counter, and heard a noise, as if there was something tearing; I went round the counter to the prisoner, and saw him taking a gown away - it had hung, on a nail: he was putting it up in his arm, and was in the act of taking it away; it had caught by a nail in taking it down, and had torn; I followed him into the court - he had come in by the back door; when he saw me coming towards him, he dropped the gown in the passage; he did not carry that into the court - I followed, and asked what business he had to take that gown; he said, "You don't mean to say such a thing of me;" I laid hold of him, and sent for the officer, to whom I gave the gown - I had seen it safe a few minutes before.
GEORGE OLLEY. I live in Wellington-street, Pentonville, and am a lint-maker. I was in the shop, and saw the prisoner enter; he enatched the gown from a nail - it caught on another nail; I called to the witness, and went for the Policeman; I did not see the prisoner drop it; he appeared in liquor.
WILLIAM PERRY (Police-constable G 25). I received the prisoner in charge; he struggled very hard, and when he could not get away, he pretended to be drunk, but I really think that he was quite sober.
Prisoner. I was going to get a handkerchief out of pledge; I had drank rather more than did me good, and stumbled, and caught the gown to save myself - I then fell out again.
GEORGE OLLEY. I saw him snatch the gown, but I could not see his face; the gown fell on the other nail, and then I saw him give another tug at it.
JURY. Q. Did you see him take it off the nail? A. Yes; he had a duplicate of a handkerchief in his hand - there is a sort of lobby at the door which he entered by; and in that lobby he dropped the gown, two or three yards from where it had hung; I saw him twisting it up.
GUILTY. Aged 20. - Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18320517-284]
1411. WILLIAM EDWARDS was indicted for stealing, on the 18th of May, 1 plane, value 2s., the goods of Thomas Capps.
THOMAS CAMROUX (Police-serjeant N 3). On the evening of the 18th of May, between seven and eight o'clock, I was in Pitfield-street, Hoxton, and saw the prisoner, with another boy, coming from Queen-street, Hoxton-market, at a gentle run - they saw me and separated; I followed, seeing something bulky under his coat; I overtook him in Ashford-street; I asked what he had got, he said Nothing - I unbuttoned his coat, and found the plane, which he said belonged to his father.
WILLIAM WORLEY. I am servant to Mr. Thomas Capps, of Old-street-road, pawnbroker. This plane is his - it was put out facing his door, on a stall - I had seen it safe that morning; I think our house is three hundred yards from Pitfield-street.
The prisoner received a good character.
GUILTY. Aged 16. - Confined Fourteen Days.
[trialAccount t18320517-285]
1412. JOHN LANGAN was indicted for stealing, on the 16th of May, 1 tea-caddy, value 25s., the goods of Catherine Duggan.
BENJAMIN TITFORD. I am in the service of Mr. David Jones, pawnbroker, of High Holborn. On the night of the 16th of May the prisoner pawned this tea-caddy with me, a little after eight o'clock.
CATHERINE DUGGAN. I am single, and lodge at my mother's, in Compton-place - this is my tea-caddy; I was out walking, between six and seven o'clock on the eveningof the 16th of May - the prisoner followed me in Tottenham-court-road, and insisted on having the caddy out of my apron - I refused him; he still followed me, and would carry it - I turned my back to look at a basket in a shop, and he was gone; I had not seen him above twice before in my life.
Prisoner. Q. Did not you give it me when we were drinking in a public-house? A. No - I refused you, and you took it out of my apron; I told you to stop while I looked at the basket, and you were gone - I only put my foot on the shop-door to ask the price of a basket; you did not return to me next morning with the duplicate and part of the money - you first told me you had the caddy, and you took me a long way, and then said you had neither caddy nor money, but I might have your body, and I had you taken - you were not so intoxicated but you knew what you were about.
COURT. Q. Where were you taking it? A. I was taking it home; when I and my cousin bought it I owed a little money at a public-house, and I left it there - I saw the prisoner the next morning, when a man went and fetched him to me from his own house, and then he took me by the shoulder, and said he had the caddy - he gave the duplicate to the Policeman.
HENRY WOODROFF (Police-constable E 53). I took the prisoner, and have the duplicate.
ELIZABETH DUGGAN. I am the prosecutrix's cousin. I was with her, and saw the prisoner trying to get the caddy from her - he would have it; she is out of place, and lives with my father and mother.
GUILTY. Aged 27. - Confined One Month.
[trialAccount t18320517-286]
Before Mr. Sergeant Arabin.
1413. ALEXANDER CLARKE was indicted for stealing, on the 16th of May, 1 saw, value 2s., the goods of Samuel Palmer.
SAMUEL PALMER. I buy and sell old wood. This saw is mine, and was taken from my yard - I missed it on the 16th of May.
JOHN JOHNSON. I am a Police-constable. I met the prisoner at a quarter before five o'clock last Wednesday afternoon, with this saw under his arm; I asked where he brought it from - he said, "Up there," and I could get no other answer from him; I took him to the watch-house - he then said he found it.
NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18320517-287]
1414. JAMES THOMPSON was indicted for stealing, on the 30th of April, 4 waistcoats, value 20s.; 8 handkerchiefs, value 20s.; 3 coats, value 3s.; 5 cravats, value 2s.; 2 pieces of linen, containing in length 26 yards each, value 50s.; 2 pairs of trousers, value 20s.; 1 pair of stockings, value 2s., and 1 portfolio, value 1s., the goods of Francois Rameau, in the dwelling-house of Mary Benson .
FRANCOIS RAMEAU (through an interpreter). I live at Mrs. Benson's, in Marylebone. On the 30th of April I returned home, between nine and ten o'clock in the evening; I had gone out at half-past nine in the morning, leaving all my property safe - when I returned I missed the property stated - the value of all I lost was 120l. - have seen nothing since but the waistcoat.
Cross-examined by MR. PRENDERGAST. Q. How was your property secured? A. My pocket-book was in my trunk, which was locked - the trunk was broken open, and the pocket-book was gone; my Christain names are Francois Ambrose, but I always sign Francois Rameau - I am not certain how it is on my passport.
COURT. Q. Are you known by the name of Francois, or have you used in this country the name of Ambrose? A. On particular occasions I call my name Ambrose.
MARY BENSON. I am landlady of the house in which the prosecutor has lodged for upwards of five years. I remember his going out on the morning of the 30th of April - I was in his room that day, about five o'clock in the afternoon - it appeared all secure, but I am not in the habit of looking over his wardrobe - the door was shut and locked; I sent a person up to his room about a quarter-past nine o'clock, to light the fire - she came down immediately, and I went up to the room; I found the door unlocked, but shut - I had the key myself; the person who got in must have opened the door with another key - I missed the property; I know nothing of the prisoner.
Cross-examined. Q. Who went into the room at a quarter-past nine o'clock? A. A person I had to assist me; I had given her my key, and she called me up - she only just went up stairs and came down again.
HENRY LAMBERT. I am in the service of a pawnbroker in Wardour-street. I have a waistcoat, pawned by the prisoner, on the 30th of April, between two and half-past four o'clock - I am certain of him; I have a recollection of its being in the afternoon; it might be about three o'clock.
EDWARD JONES (Police-constable D 16). On the night of the 30th of April I was on duty near Barlow-street, and an officer told me of a robbery at No. 7, Little Barlow-street, where Mrs. Benson lives - I went there, and found the prosecutor's room in a disturbed state; I went to give notice to the inspector - several persons were about, who pointed out the prisoner as a man who had been lurking about the house - I walked after him; he turned a street, and ran off through several streets; I followed, and took him - I asked him why he ran; he said he wanted to go home - this was past eleven o'clock; I took him to the station, and found on him the duplicate of the waistcoat which had been pawned that day for 7s., in the name of John Thompson, No. 3, Chapel-street.
THOMAS GAYDON. I live opposite Mrs. Benson's; I saw the prisoner that evening walking about several times, and pointed him out to the officer.
Cross-examined. Q.When did you see him? A. About half-past nine o'clock was the first time - he was about, I dare say, for half an hour, and then went away; I took particular notice of him, by his watching round the house.
FRANCOIS RAMEAU. This is my waistcoat.
Cross-examined. Q. How long have you had it? A. About three years; I do not know that I had seen it that day, I had two or three days before - I left it in the cupboard.
Prisoner. The pawnbroker has stated quite false; it was pawned between twelve and one o'clock.
GUILTY of stealing the waistcoat only. Aged 26.
Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18320517-288]
1415. JOHN HYDE and SARAH BAYLEY were indicted for feloniously assaulting Guy Pickering, on the 28th of April; putting him in fear, and taking from his person, and against his will, 1 pocket-book, value 2s.; 2 sovereigns, 1 half-crown, 4 shillings, and 1 sixpence, his property.
GUY PICKERING. I live in Little Moorfields, and am a chemist and druggist. On the 28th of April, at twelve o'clock at night, I was in Wentworth-street; I saw Bayley at a door; she said "Where are you going my dear" - I said "What is that to you?" I was going away from her; a crush of five or six men and women then came from across the street, and crushed me into a passage; I did not see Bayley then - I was struck three or four times, and was crushed on through a narrow passage into a yard full of carts and waggons; when I got there Hyde struck me through the hat, over the head, I think with his fist; it was a heavy blow; I called out Murder! and got into a corner of the yard; I said, I would blow the first man's brains out who came near handy, but I had no pistol - the Policeman came to my assistance, and Hyde was taken to the station; Bayley was brought in in about two hours, and two or three other women - I had remained at the station, and I recognized her as the person who met me in the street; I had two glasses of gin and water, one at eight o'clock, and the other at half-past eleven, in Mile-end-road; I had been with Mr. Harris, of Morgan-street, and left the public-house at half-past eleven - I had then two sovereigns, a half-crown, a sixpence, and four shillings, in my right hand pocket - I paid one shilling then for two glasses of gin and water, and counted my money - this was half an hour before I went to Wentworth-street; I missed my money in the yard where the carts and waggons were; and I had a memorandum-book cut out of my pocket - I missed my money about three minutes after Hyde struck me; I did not miss my book till ten o'clock the next day, and my right hand coat pocket was cut - I had not been with any other person after I left the station-house; I observed my right hand coat pocket was cut, and the book was gone - I staid at the station-house two hours, and then went home; I was not conscious at the time of losing my money; I was defending myself; I had not spoken to any one from the time I counted my money till I lost it - I was sober.
Hyde. I was coming down the street and heard a cry of Murder! I went into the yard with a light, and asked what was the matter with you. Witness. He did not come till after the officer came.
HENRY BOLTON (Police-constable H 87). I was on duty in Wentworth-street, and heard a noise in the yard -I went to the gates; they were fastened - they are rail gates; you can see through them; there is an access to the yard through Mr. Sheen's house; I went through Wass' shop, and found the prosecutor and Hyde there - the prosecutor said, "Take this man into custody for robbing me;" I went into the yard; they were talking, but I cannot say what about - Hyde seemed much exaxperated and endeavoured to strike the prosecutor, which I preveuted - the prosecutor had been drinking, but he walked on before us, and seemed to know what he was doing; there are two houses in the yard - Hyde lives in one of them; the prosecutor could have gone through the passage of Sheen's house, which is open, and they take in lodgers - I found only 1s. 5d. on Hyde - I got a key, and searched his house, but found nothing; I saw some dust or dry dirt on the prosecutor's side.
JOHN WHITE (Police-constable H 180). I received information from Bolton, and took Bayley at the end of Rose-lane, with two other females, an hour and a half after this happened - the prosecutor said she was the woman - nothing was found on her.
MARY WASS. I am the wife of Leonard Wass - we keep a shop in Wentworth-street. About half-past twelve o'clock I heard a cry of Murder! I went to the door, and Hyde came to me; he went to Mrs. Sheen, who desired him to get a light, and see what was amiss in the yard; he came to me, and I gave him a light; there is a passage through Sheen's house, for the convenience of persons living in the yard - Hyde lives in one; that is the passage which the prosecutor describes as being forced through; my house is a part of Sheen's; I could not hear any one pass through that passage, as there is a tenement between - I went to the yard with another candle, after Hyde; I saw the prosecutor there, holding a bridle, which was tied to the van - his dress was disorded; the Policeman was with him - before I left my house I heard a voice my"Murder! Murder! I will shoot, I will, by G-D."
AMELIA FLEMING. I was in my son's shop, in Wentworth-street; I heard a cry of Murder! I went out - I Hyde, and Mrs. Wass went to the gate of the yard; I heard Hyde say, "Who the hell are you? I will get a candle, and see;" I could not see the prosecutor.
COURT to GUY PICKERING. Q. What light was there at the time you were struck? A.There are large gates, with rails on the top - I had my back towards the light from the street; as Hyde came towards me from the inner part of the yard, I could see his face -I am positive it was him; when I cried Murder! he went off directly; he was a minutes or two in my presence before he struck me - I saw him coming to strike me, and I struck to keep him off - I am not mistaken in his person.
HENRY BOLTON re-examined. Q. Does it appear to you that in the spot in which the prosecutor was he could discover, by the light of the street, the person and features of a man? A. Yes; they are rail gates - there is a gas-light opposite, and with his back to the gates the light would he shown on the face of a person opposite -Hyde had a light.
MARY WASS. When I got to the yard I could not see who it was; I was told the prosecutor produced a sovereign at the station-house.
GUY PICKERING. I know I did - that was in my other pocket - it belonged to a brother of mine; I had taken it that day.
JURY. Q. How many persons were in the yard? A. At first there were five or six - the passage is about two feet and a half wide; I have no doubt but two or three persons followed me; I could not see them; I made towards the light; the gates were shut, and the prisoners followed me.
NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18320517-289]
1416. JOSEPH SEAGER was indicted for feloniously breaking and entering the dwelling-house of Thomas Hardwick, on the 22nd of April, and stealing 10 penknives, value 30s.; 1 pair of razors and strop, value 16s.; 1 case of ladies' instruments, value 16s., and 2 nail-knives, value 5s., the goods of John Joseph Mechi; and 2 pictures, value 2s. 6d., the goods of Thomas Hardwick; and HANNAH SEAGER was indicted for feloniously receiving the same, well knowing them to have been stolen.
The same evidence was given in this case as on the former trial of the prisoners (see page 523).
NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18320517-290]
1417. BRIDGET MARS was indicted for feloniously assaulting Sarah Brennan, on the 24th of April, at St. Giles in the Fields, putting her in fear, and taking from her person, and against her will, 1 shawl, value 5s., the goods of Michael Brennan .
SARAH BRENNAN. I am the wife of Michael Brennan; we live in Monmouth-street. On the night of the 24th of April I left a public-house, near St. Giles' church, between ten and eleven o'clock - Maria Mayne was with me; I saw the prisoner with two other women; when I got some distance I received a blow on the back of my neck - I fell; I saw the prisoner - she took a shawl from me and ran away; I called Murder! and Police! and she was stopped.
Prisoner. Q. Were there any other persons with you? A. Yes, there were six of us; my husband and others - I saw you in the public-house - I did not speak to you then - I did not turn and say, "Here is that prostitute following me;" I did not see any fight with you or any of the company; I saw you tearing a woman's bonnet in the public-house, and we went out in consequence of that; you had no cap on, nor bonnet - I had not observed the prisoner following me; I did not see any one with her when I was knocked down.
MARIA MAYNE. I am the wife of Stephen Mayne, we live in Porter-street, Newport-market. I left the public-house with the prosecutrix, and was on my way home - I did not perceive that the prisoner was following us, till she struck the prosecutrix; there were two women with her - I saw her strike the prosecutrix, and she fell from the blow; the prisoner snatched her shawl from her shoulders, and threw it to the two women, who ran up one of the turnings towards St. Giles' - I believe it is George-street; one of the women caught the shawl; it did not fall I have not said before that it did; I said I believed it was Plumtree-street, but it was not; the prisoner ran off - I called Police! and she was stopped.
Prisoner. I did not get half a yard away; we both fell together. Witness. No; the prisoner did not fall - I said I should know one of the women who was with her.
JAMES STEWARD. I am a boot-maker, and live in Rose-street, Long-acre; I was two or three yards behind the prosecutrix - I saw the prisoner make a rush at her; she knocked her down, snatched her shawl, and ran off with it; I pursued, and she threw it to two girls on the pavement - she was running in the road, and did not stop for a moment - I caught her about two hundred yards off - she said, "Sir, what have I done?" I said,"Knocked a woman down, and stolen her shawl" - she begged of me to let her go; I said I would not till the persons came up, which they did in about two minutes - a Policeman came up, we stated the affair to him and he took her; I had been at the public-house with the prosecutrix and her husband; there was a dance there - I do not remember seeing the prisoner there; there was some disturbance, but I do not know what; there had been no insult to the prisoner by any of our party; I did not see any one strike her at all, or call her an improper name.
Prisoner. Q. Did you not all quarrel together in the public-house, and strike me and use me very shamefully? A. I do not recollect seeing you at all - there was a little disturbance about dancing, and the prosecutrix said it was not a fit place for us to be in, and we went out.
Prisoner's Defence. These people and I were at the public-house; we had some words, and they all struck me as I was coming down the passage - I then came down the street, and saw the prosecutrix and her friends - she said,"Here is the street-walker, whom we had words with in the public-house;" I said I was no such thing - she then struck me; I defended myself as well as I could - all her friends were about her; I had my cap and handkerchief and all torn off - as to the shawl I am innocent; I had no person with me.
SARAH BRENNAN. Upon my solemn, oath I did not strike her, nor did she fall.
The prisoner received a good character.
GUILTY of stealing from the person only. Aged 18.
Recommended to Mercy. - Confined Three Months.
